## intake-2119#record — DualPath: Breaking the Storage Bandwidth Bottleneck in Agentic LLM Inference

- **URL**: https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.21548
- **Verification**: dive-verified
- **Verdict**: adopt_patterns (novelty medium, relevance medium; categories: inference_serving, kv_cache, agent_architecture)
- **Disposition**: integrated
- **Expanded from**: intake-2116#record

### Claims (as verified)

0. WORKLOAD (vendor-reported, DeepSeek's own agentic-RL coding traces): mean 157 rounds, mean context 32.7k tokens, mean append 429 tokens, giving a 98.7% KV-cache hit rate; cache-compute ratio ~22 GB/PFLOP for DeepSeek-V3.2 (FP8 KV, append 429), and 4.8-5.8 (V3) up to 117-267 (Qwen2.5-32B FP16) GB/PFLOP across 16K-64K contexts in Table 1. Hit rates are derived per trace set in Table 2 (MaxLen 32K/48K/64K: append 608/474/429 over context 17183/25120/32721, i.e. ~96.5/98.1/98.7% by arithmetic on those columns).
   - *narrowed*: I/O-bound holds for single-storage-NIC nodes and MLA/FP8 KV at 98.7% hit; ratio varies 50x across models (Table 1).
   - anchor `Section 3 para 3` @ arXiv 2602.21548v2 (26 Feb 2026), read from s1/dualpath/text.txt: "Our trace collected from representative coding tasks shows the mean number of rounds is 157, demonstrating the tendency of LLMs to engage in multi-turn interactions. The average context length is 32.7k, while the append length mean is only 429, which means a KV-Cache hit rate of 98.7%."
   - anchor `Section 3 para 3` @ arXiv 2602.21548v2 (26 Feb 2026), read from s1/dualpath/text.txt: "the cache-compute ratio, defined as the ratio of KV-Cache to load and the computation needed, is approximately 22 GB/PFLOP for DeepSeek-V3.2"
   - anchor `Table 2` @ arXiv 2602.21548v2 (26 Feb 2026), read from s1/dualpath/text.txt: "MaxLen Turns Append Gen Total Context 32K 60 608 148 28639 17183 48K 106 474 172 42607 25120 64K 157 429 176 55958 32721"
1. MECHANISM: dual-path KV loading in a prefill/decode-disaggregated, layerwise-prefill system. The usual path reads hit KV from storage into the prefill engine's DRAM buffer; the added path reads it into the DECODE engine's DRAM buffer and streams it layer by layer to the prefill engine over the RDMA compute network. Requires PD disaggregation, per-engine DRAM buffers, a storage NIC per node (3FS) and an RDMA fabric; the bottleneck it removes is saturated prefill-side storage-NIC bandwidth with idle decode-side NICs.
   - *unaffected*: Mechanism confirmed; prerequisites (PD disagg, RDMA, DRAM buffers, 3FS) now stated.
   - anchor `Section 4.1` @ arXiv 2602.21548v2 (26 Feb 2026), read from s1/dualpath/text.txt: "DualPath introduces a novel storage-to-decode path, allowing KV-Cache to be loaded first into a decode engine and then transferred to the prefill engine via high-bandwidth RDMA over the compute network."
2. TRAFFIC ISOLATION: all GPU traffic, including local H2D/D2H, goes through the GPU's paired compute NIC (GPUDirect RDMA) so InfiniBand virtual-lane arbitration can give model-communication traffic ~99% priority; cudaMemcpyAsync submission costs ~5-7 us versus ~1 us per RDMA write work request. Hardware-specific (IB VLs / RoCE TC+DSCP).
   - *unaffected*: Confirmed; hardware-specific (IB VLs).
   - anchor `Section 5.1` @ arXiv 2602.21548v2 (26 Feb 2026), read from s1/dualpath/text.txt: "We configure the VL arbiters of all network switches and NICs with a weighted round-robin policy that reserves approximately 99% of total bandwidth to high-priority VL."
3. PE (prefill-engine) INTER-ENGINE SCHEDULING (Algorithm 1): FIFO queue; engines reporting unfinished-token count tok_e and node disk-read queue length are split into overloaded (tok_e > beta, skipped), short-read-queue (read_q <= alpha) and long-read-queue; assign each request to the min-tok_e engine in the short-queue class, else the long-queue class. alpha = tokens readable in 3 s, beta = tokens one GPU processes in 5 s, both profiled in advance.
   - *narrowed*: Disk-read-queue length is a PE-side/read-side signal only; DE side uses token+request count+HBM.
   - anchor `Section 6.1 PE Scheduling` @ arXiv 2602.21548v2 (26 Feb 2026), read from s1/dualpath/text.txt: "We do not assign new requests to overloaded engines. Second-category engines are prioritized over third-category engines because they reside on nodes with shorter disk reading queues, and lack of subsequent requests would easily lead to storage NIC underutilization."
   - anchor `Section 6.1 (load proxy)` @ arXiv 2602.21548v2 (26 Feb 2026), read from s1/dualpath/text.txt: "GPU load, disk read load, and network load are all strongly correlated with token count. We therefore use token count as a proxy and aim to balance it across engines."
   - anchor `Appendix A.4` @ arXiv 2602.21548v2 (26 Feb 2026), read from s1/dualpath/text.txt: "The short reading queue threshold α (described in § 6 ) is set to the number of tokens we can read during 3 seconds, and the unfinished token upper limit β is set to the number of tokens one GPU can process for 5 seconds."
4. DE (decode-engine) SCHEDULING is two-level, not FIFO: (1) a global queue drains into per-group private queues, each request going to the group whose summed tok_e is smallest; (2) within a group, count how many queue-head requests fit in the group's remaining HBM (set R), set Z = 1.05 x (sum of len over R + sum of tok_e over the group) / |group|, then place each head request on a DE with enough free HBM, preferring DEs where tok_e + len(r) <= Z and, among those, the one with MINIMUM request count seq_e; if none is under Z, pick the min-tok_e DE (to reduce HBM exhaustion and preemption risk). No steering by request length is stated.
   - *reattributed*: The DeepInfer Q&A short/long steering is not DualPath text; DualPath has two-dimension balance with a token threshold Z=1.05 x mean.
   - anchor `Section 6.1 DE Scheduling (first para)` @ arXiv 2602.21548v2 (26 Feb 2026), read from s1/dualpath/text.txt: "DE scheduling is two-level and does not preserve global FIFO. There is a global waiting queue and a private queue per DE engine group."
   - anchor `Section 6.1 DE Scheduling Phase 2` @ arXiv 2602.21548v2 (26 Feb 2026), read from s1/dualpath/text.txt: "Among DEs with sufficient remaining HBM for the request, we partition into (1) high-token DEs"
   - anchor `Section 6.1 DE Scheduling Phase 2` @ arXiv 2602.21548v2 (26 Feb 2026), read from s1/dualpath/text.txt: "We prefer category (2) to keep token count balanced; category (1) DEs already have higher GPU and NIC pressure."
   - anchor `Section 6.1 DE Scheduling Phase 2` @ arXiv 2602.21548v2 (26 Feb 2026), read from s1/dualpath/text.txt: "to balance request count; if category (2) is empty we choose the DE with minimum"
   - anchor `Section 6.1 DE Scheduling Phase 2` @ arXiv 2602.21548v2 (26 Feb 2026), read from s1/dualpath/text.txt: "If no DE has sufficient HBM, the fetch ends and already-assigned requests are returned."
5. ATTRIBUTION CHECK: the DeepInfer Q&A says its Decode Load Balancer 'sends short requests to GPUs with low concurrency but large total KV cache length, and long requests to GPUs with small total KV cache length' per 'DualPath's intra-group scheduling'. The two-dimension balance (request count AND total token/KV length) IS in DualPath (DE Phase 2, 'within a group'); the explicit short-to-high-KV / long-to-low-KV steering is NOT stated in the paper. The nearest effect is emergent: a long request breaks the tok_e + len <= Z test on loaded DEs and so lands on lighter ones, while a short request passes everywhere and goes to the min-seq_e DE regardless of its KV total. Paper's rationale is NIC and GPU load (token count as proxy) plus HBM fit, not attention cost.
6. INTRA-ENGINE scheduling (Section 6.2) applies ONLY to prefill engines: FIFO packing under a per-forward attention-time 'compute quota' (300 ms in the experiments), with binary search on the chunk length bsz' for the last request; time predicted from (cached, bsz) pairs by a profiled fit. 'DEs always place all requests into the forward batch.' (The DeepInfer Q&A's prefill compute_quota_limit description corresponds to this section, not to its decode balancer.)
   - anchor `Section 6.2` @ arXiv 2602.21548v2 (26 Feb 2026), read from s1/dualpath/text.txt: "Only PEs require intra-engine scheduling, as DEs always place all requests into the forward batch."
7. OFFLINE RESULTS (vendor-reported; baseline 'Basic' = the authors' own unmodified in-house framework; 'Oracle' bypasses all KV I/O): up to 1.87x lower job completion time than Basic on DS-660B (2P4D, 64K MAL, up to 2048 agents); up to 1.78x on DS-27B (1P1D) which remains 1.09-1.85x slower than Oracle; P/D-ratio sweep on DS-27B averages 1.64x (max 2.46x); append-length sweep 1.82-1.99x. Hopper GPUs, 400 Gbps storage NIC per node, 3FS with no DRAM cache, speculative decoding disabled.
   - anchor `Section 7.3` @ arXiv 2602.21548v2 (26 Feb 2026), read from s1/dualpath/text.txt: "On DS 660B, DualPath achieves up to 1.87 × over Basic"
   - anchor `Section 7.2 Baselines` @ arXiv 2602.21548v2 (26 Feb 2026), read from s1/dualpath/text.txt: "Comparing DualPath and SGL(MC) is unfair due to implementation differences. Therefore, we only report performance improvements from Basic to Ours."
   - anchor `Appendix A.4` @ arXiv 2602.21548v2 (26 Feb 2026), read from s1/dualpath/text.txt: "Speculative decoding is disabled for all settings."
8. ONLINE RESULTS (vendor-reported, SLO TTFT <= 4 s and TPOT <= 50 ms, Poisson agent arrivals): agent-arrival-rate capacity 1.67x (DS-27B) and 2.25x (DS-660B) over Basic; the abstract's 'average 1.96x' equals the mean of those two (arithmetic observation, the paper does not say so). TPOT not worse than Basic. SGLang(HiCache+Mooncake) is NOT a like-for-like comparison: the authors report only Basic-to-Ours gains.
   - anchor `Section 7.4` @ arXiv 2602.21548v2 (26 Feb 2026), read from s1/dualpath/text.txt: "DualPath achieves higher APS capacity than Basic (1.67 × for DS 27B, 2.25 × for DS 660B)."
9. ABLATION (DS-660B, 64K, 1024/2048 agents, cumulative JCT reduction vs Basic): layerwise prefill -17.21%, plus dual-path loading -38.19%, plus the scheduling algorithm -45.62% (so scheduling adds ~7.4 points ON TOP of dual-path loading; the DE routing rule alone is not isolated). Storage-NIC max/avg traffic ratio 1.18 vs 1.53 for round-robin; attention-time max/avg across an EP group 1.06 early in the run.
   - anchor `Section 7.5` @ arXiv 2602.21548v2 (26 Feb 2026), read from s1/dualpath/text.txt: "achieves the best performance, reducing JCT by 45.62 % compared to Basic"
   - anchor `Section 7.5 Load Balance` @ arXiv 2602.21548v2 (26 Feb 2026), read from s1/dualpath/text.txt: "our scheduling algorithm improves load balance from 1.53 to 1.18 compared to round robin scheduling"
10. LIMITS AS STATED: KV hits counted only within a trajectory with no eviction (client-computed hit length); the 48P96D scale test shows near-linear scaling but 'does not demonstrate additional ... gains compared with multiple small-scale units'; scheduler uses < 10 CPU cores; combining with a middle DRAM cache gives 'marginal' gain; the authors say per-request read-side splitting across both paths is future work.
   - anchor `Appendix A.4` @ arXiv 2602.21548v2 (26 Feb 2026), read from s1/dualpath/text.txt: "we limit the KV-Cache hits to only occur within a trajectory, and the hit length is calculated in the client because no eviction is needed."
   - anchor `Section 9 Related Work` @ arXiv 2602.21548v2 (26 Feb 2026), read from s1/dualpath/text.txt: "DualPath can also be combined with a middle DRAM cache, but the performance gain is marginal."
   - anchor `Section 7.6` @ arXiv 2602.21548v2 (26 Feb 2026), read from s1/dualpath/text.txt: "Across all experiments, scheduler CPU usage remains below 10 cores"
11. WORKING SET: approximated as lambda x T_bar x total_len_avg / 2 (arrival rate x mean job completion time x mean length / 2); for DS-660B 69 GB at 0.1 APS to 681 GB at 0.45 APS, and grows ~r^2 if tool-call latency stretches JCT by r. Sizing rule for a host-RAM KV tier (cf. --cache-ram).
   - anchor `Section 8.2` @ arXiv 2602.21548v2 (26 Feb 2026), read from s1/dualpath/text.txt: "the working set of the KV-Cache can thus be approximated as"
   - anchor `Section 8.2` @ arXiv 2602.21548v2 (26 Feb 2026), read from s1/dualpath/text.txt: "In our setting of DS 660B serving, this value of DualPath ranges from 69 GB at APS 0.1 to 681 GB at APS 0.45."

### Dive corrections

- 2026-10-20 (wave 1): Stage 1 had not read DE scheduling; now read. The DeepInfer-cited rule is the DE Phase 2 within-group rule (token threshold Z then min request count). The Q&A short-to-high-KV steering is NOT in DualPath. Stage-1 headline narrowed: 1.87x is vs the authors own Basic on DS-660B, the 1.96x average = mean of 1.67x and 2.25x APS capacity; scheduling adds ~7.4 JCT points on top of dual-path loading and is not isolated. 2026-10-20 second reader (independent agent): re-find 25/25; support yes 10 / partial 1 / no 0. Disagreement carried to Stage 3: claim 7 ties the 1.87x to a 64K MAL / 2048-agent operating point the text does not state (it says 'up to 1.87x across batch sizes and MALs').

### Verdict

Cite DualPath for (a) the token-count-proxy, two-dimension (token + request count) decode-side routing rule with the Z=1.05 x mean threshold, (b) the cache-compute-ratio metric and its 50x model spread (Table 1), (c) the working-set formula. Do NOT cite it for the DeepInfer Q&A claim that short requests go to high-KV GPUs and long requests to low-KV GPUs: that steering is not in the paper (intake-2116#record reading is a reattribution). Do NOT carry the 1.87x/1.96x as expected EPYC gains: they are vendor-reported on a storage-NIC-bound multi-node cluster, against the authors own baseline, and the routing rule is not ablated in isolation. Dual-path loading itself has no second link to exploit on one host (shared PCIe).

### Linked handoffs and actions

- updated `within-role-placement-state-machine.md`
- updated `heterogeneous-slot-fabric-residency.md`
- updated `kv-unified-stack-rollout.md`
- Recommended action (dive proposal, not an instruction): Operator-review candidate: pin the decode-side group scheduling algorithm the DeepInfer Q&A cites and judge it for np-slot assignment and CPU/GPU request routing.

## intake-2120#record — DeepSelect: TopK kernels for DeepSeek Sparse Attention (DSA) and samplers

- **URL**: https://github.com/deepseek-ai/DeepSelect
- **Verification**: dive-verified
- **Verdict**: adopt_patterns (novelty medium, relevance medium; categories: hardware_optimization, inference_serving, local_inference)
- **Disposition**: integrated
- **Expanded from**: intake-2116#record

### Claims (as verified)

0. Algorithm (settled): a single-pass threshold-filter with periodic compaction, not a multi-pass whole-row radix histogram. It scans fixed-size blocks in a pseudo-random block order, appends only elements > the current k-th-largest threshold T to a shared-memory candidate buffer, and when the buffer reaches k+B2 runs a radix-select TopK on the buffer to cut it back to k and raise T. Every input element is read from global memory once, in contiguous blocks; extra space is O(k+B+B2); the doc derives an EXPECTED bound E[W] <= (1+k/B2)(k+B+B2)H_m on elements processed by the radix stage (assumes distinct elements and a uniform random block permutation independent of the input). Suggested config for k=512 is B=B2=1024.
   - *narrowed*: Stage 1 said "bandwidth-oriented TopK"; algorithm is threshold-filter + compaction, radix only on the small buffer
   - anchor `docs/DeepSelect-deep-dive.md §Algorithm ¶3 @ bfa4507` @ bfa4507d935f17ebfc3d0f00ff7d3c9a4d0e5c18: "run radix-select-based TopK in shared memory (or another TopK algorithm) to reduce it back to $`k`$ elements"
   - anchor `docs/DeepSelect-deep-dive.md §Algorithm Notes @ bfa4507` @ bfa4507d935f17ebfc3d0f00ff7d3c9a4d0e5c18: "Every element of $`x`$ is read exactly once, with accesses performed in contiguous blocks of size $`B`$."
   - anchor `docs/DeepSelect-deep-dive.md §Algorithm ¶5 @ bfa4507` @ bfa4507d935f17ebfc3d0f00ff7d3c9a4d0e5c18: "To avoid performance degradation on unfavorable inputs, blocks are processed in a random order."
1. Framing (settled): the kernel is benchmarked as effective memory bandwidth (TB/s) because TopK does no floating-point work; the design goal is to stream the row once at HBM rate and keep selection work on an O(k) candidate set.
   - *unaffected*: Stage-1 claim 3 (measured as effective memory bandwidth): confirmed at README Performance section; main filled the empty dive note from the dive's CONFIRMED verdict.
   - anchor `README.md §Performance @ bfa4507` @ bfa4507d935f17ebfc3d0f00ff7d3c9a4d0e5c18: "TopK does no floating-point math, so a FLOP rate would not be meaningful here."
2. Supported cases: bf16 lightning-indexer rows (any batch, any row length, topk <= 4096, tuned for 512) on CUDA and Ascend; fp32 ~128K-vocab sampler rows on CUDA only (Ascend is bf16-only). Row stride must be aligned (get_stride_requirement); output index dtype int32/int64 (Ascend int32 only); sorted-by-value output only for fp32 on CUDA.
   - *unaffected*: fp32 sampler CUDA-only added
   - anchor `README.md §Sampling Scenario @ bfa4507` @ bfa4507d935f17ebfc3d0f00ff7d3c9a4d0e5c18: "Currently, only the CUDA implementation supports `torch.float32` as the input dtype."
3. Reported performance: README says 2-20x vs torch.topk; the text names no GPU and gives no numbers, the figures are PNG bar charts (bf16 topk=512 at batch 6/512/4096 x vocab 16K..1M; fp32 vocab=129280 topk=512 at batch 6..4096). Approximate chart reading (this dive, not a source number): bf16 batch 4096 vocab 1M ~5.3 TB/s vs ~0.27 for torch.topk (~20x); batch 512 vocab 64K ~2.0 vs ~0.25; batch 6 vocab 1M ~0.55 vs ~0.13; fp32 batch 4096 ~2.8 vs ~1.15 TB/s (~2.5x). The achieved TB/s implies HBM-class hardware, which the source does not name. Vendor-reported, not reproducible on our hardware.
   - *narrowed*: 2-20x is a sweep envelope; hardware not named; fp32 sampler ratio ~2.5x
   - anchor `README.md intro @ bfa4507` @ bfa4507d935f17ebfc3d0f00ff7d3c9a4d0e5c18: "achieves 2 ~ 20x speedup compared to vanilla `torch.topk`"
4. Variable-length rows: per-row exclusive upper bound `end` (int32, per batch row); rows with end <= topk are gathered first and padded with value_oob_fill_value / idx_oob_fill_value. `begin` and `hint` exist in the signature but assert as unsupported. Index-only output (return_value=False, README says ~10% faster) and optional sorted_index (costs performance). NaN is always checked and by default trap()-aborts the kernel (CUDA skips the check for rows with length <= topk).
   - *narrowed*: begin/hint unsupported; NaN trap aborts by default
   - anchor `deep_select/interface.py (topk body) @ bfa4507` @ bfa4507d935f17ebfc3d0f00ff7d3c9a4d0e5c18: "assert begin is None, "`begin` is not supported now""
   - anchor `README.md §Variable-length rows @ bfa4507` @ bfa4507d935f17ebfc3d0f00ff7d3c9a4d0e5c18: "`end` sets a per-row upper bound (exclusive)."
5. Small-batch scheduling (CUDA only): when the batch cannot fill the SMs (or the last wave is sparse), a row is split across the SMs of a thread-block cluster; each CTA takes the top-k of its part and sends it to CTA0, which selects the final top-k.
   - anchor `docs/DeepSelect-deep-dive.md §Implementation last ¶ @ bfa4507` @ bfa4507d935f17ebfc3d0f00ff7d3c9a4d0e5c18: "we use Threadblock Cluster to split the computation of a sequence across multiple Streaming Multiprocessors (SMs)"
6. GPU-specific constant-factor tricks (bit-mask + ffs compaction, PTX set/prmt/dp4a, integer add via denormal fp32 add, TMA loads, warp prefix sums) carry no direct CPU meaning; the portable content is the algorithm shape of claims 0 and 5.

### Dive corrections

- 2026-10-20 (wave 1): algorithm settled as threshold-filter+compaction (not multi-pass radix); hardware for the 2-20x is unnamed; our CPU top_k is partial_sort (not full sort) but single-thread at nrows=1; HIP path may fall to bitonic argsort capped at 1024 cols. 2026-10-20 second reader (independent agent): re-find 9/9; support yes 6 / partial 0 / no 0; corrections applied: none required.

### Verdict

Cite as vendor-reported algorithm evidence: one streaming read + threshold filter + O(k) compaction; benchmark metric is bandwidth. Do NOT carry 2-20x as an expectation for our shapes, and do NOT assume top-k is a material fraction of DS41 decode: no such measurement exists (DS41-C7a table has no top_k row). What is code-verified on our side: CPU GGML_OP_TOP_K is std::partial_sort, F32-only, one row per thread (single-thread at decode nrows=1); sampler top-k is already a histogram pass. First deciding measurement is A1 (DS41-C8 profile of lid_top_k).

### Linked handoffs and actions

- updated `deepseek-v41-flash-evaluation.md`
- updated `kv-unified-stack-rollout.md`
- Recommended action (dive proposal, not an instruction): Operator-review candidate: read the algorithm deep-dive and judge a CPU (AVX-512) / HIP port for the DS41 indexer top-k after profiling its share of decode.

### Claims (as verified)

DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash-1017 (HF 1bae0e1088993b9b82de08f59de5be5a34e42170, created 2026-10-17T02:40Z, last modified 2026-10-17T05:46Z, MIT) is described by its card as a minor update to DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash with improvements in reasoning, coding, agentic and visual benchmarks plus 'reduced task execution time and output length'; the card gives NO figure for execution time or output length.

The 1017 card ships no Jinja chat template and points to deepseek-recipe for production prompt rendering; its benchmark table is vendor-reported (1017 vs September vs K3 / GLM-5.3 / GPT-5.6 Sol / Opus 5) and its 'September' column disagrees with the September card on at least NL2Repo (65.4 vs 64.0) and the Apex row (64.6 vs 65.6 under a different benchmark name).

Benchmark table found inconsistent with the September card on two rows. No generation_config.json exists. 2026-10-20 second reader (independent agent): re-find 7/7; support yes 6 / partial 0 / no 0; corrections applied: none required.

Do NOT carry: the benchmark numbers as quality evidence for our quant (vendor-reported, conditions unstated, inconsistent with the September card), 'shorter outputs' as a measured effect, or the idea that 1017 is a pure weight swap at the reference-code level. Whether act_quant(o) or the index_k change affects our port's outputs is unmeasured.

## intake-2122#record — FlashMLA: MLA / DSA sparse attention kernels with FP8 and FP4 KV formats (DeepSeek-V4.1)

- **URL**: https://github.com/deepseek-ai/FlashMLA
- **Verification**: dive-verified
- **Verdict**: adopt_patterns (novelty medium, relevance medium; categories: inference_serving, kv_cache, hardware_optimization, quantization)
- **Disposition**: integrated
- **Expanded from**: intake-2116#record

### Claims (as verified)

0. V4.1 FP8 KV layout (528 B/token, settled): 512 B of float8_e4m3 covering ALL 512 dims (448 NoPE + 64 RoPE, RoPE is quantized too, no bf16 part) followed by 16 B of float8_e8m0 scales, one per 32 consecutive values (16 tiles = 14 NoPE + 2 RoPE). Quantizer (tests/quant.py): per-tile scale = 2^ceil(log2(max(amax/448, 1e-4))) i.e. a power of two; dequant = e4m3 value x e8m0 scale. Data and scales are adjacent PER TOKEN (changed from the older per-block data-then-scales layout in the 2026-09-30 release).
   - *narrowed*: bf16 KV unsupported confirmed; FP4 cannot be the sole KV store
   - anchor `README.md §FP8 / FP4 KV Cache Format @ 0f11e17` @ 0f11e178da1f67becb087a324b400854931ec3e3: "The last 16 bytes are `float8_e8m0` scales, one for every 32 consecutive `float8_e4m3` values."
   - anchor `tests/quant.py:L26-L27 @ 0f11e17` @ 0f11e178da1f67becb087a324b400854931ec3e3: "return torch.pow(2, torch.clamp_min(scales_inv, 1e-4).log2().ceil()).to(out_dtype)"
1. V4.1 FP4 KV layout (288 B/token, settled): 256 B of packed e2m1 (512 values, two per byte, even-indexed element in the LOW nibble) followed by 32 B of float8_e4m3 scales, one per 16 values (32 tiles, RoPE included). Quantizer: scale = clamp(amax/6, 2^-9, 448) rounded to e4m3 (not a power of two), values rounded to nearest e2m1 ties-to-even saturating at +-6, a NaN/inf tile poisons its scale; no per-tensor scale. The FP4 format is only valid as an EXTRA cache next to an FP8 main cache (interface docstring), not as the sole KV store.
   - *narrowed*: fp4 288 B format only valid as extra cache beside the FP8 main cache; scale rules and nibble order now specified
   - anchor `README.md §FP8 / FP4 KV Cache Format @ 0f11e17` @ 0f11e178da1f67becb087a324b400854931ec3e3: "The last 32 bytes are `float8_e4m3` scales, one for every 16 consecutive `e2m1` values. This format is only valid for `extra_k_cache` when `k_cache` is in the V4.1 format."
   - anchor `tests/quant.py:L114 @ 0f11e17` @ 0f11e178da1f67becb087a324b400854931ec3e3: "scale = torch.clamp(amax / 6.0, 2.0**-9, 448.0).to(torch.float8_e4m3fn)"
2. Sparse indices and per-query controls: decode `indices` [b, s_q, topk] int32 hold page_block*page_block_size + offset, -1 = invalid (decode checks only -1 and does no upper-bound check; prefill treats -1 or >= s_kv as invalid), so block_table and cache_seqlens are ignored. topk_length / extra_topk_length cap how many leading indices each batch row (decode) or query token (prefill) uses. attn_sink [h_q] fp32 exists in BOTH decode and prefill: output is scaled by exp(lse)/(exp(lse)+exp(sink)) and returned lse is unaffected. Decode also accepts an extra_k_cache + extra_indices pair attended in addition to the main cache. Constraints: MQA (h_k=1), head_dim = head_dim_v = 512, h_q 64 or 128, bf16 KV unsupported.
   - *narrowed*: attn_sink and topk_length are present in decode as well as prefill; decode checks only -1
   - anchor `README.md §indices Tensor @ 0f11e17` @ 0f11e178da1f67becb087a324b400854931ec3e3: "the kernel does not use the `block_table` parameter"
   - anchor `flash_mla/flash_mla_interface.py:L67-L71,L105 @ 0f11e17` @ 0f11e178da1f67becb087a324b400854931ec3e3: "the final output will be scaled by exp(lse) / (exp(lse) + exp(attn_sink))"
3. Decode split/scheduling: get_mla_metadata() returns an empty placeholder; the real metadata (tile_scheduler_metadata int32 [num_sm_parts, 8] and num_splits int32 [batch+1]) is generated inside the first flash_mla_with_kvcache call and stored in the same object. It may be reused only while tensor shapes and the VALUES of topk_length/extra_topk_length are unchanged (not checked: stale values silently reuse stale split-KV schedule). enable_batch_invariant=True disables split-KV so results do not depend on batch partition. The split algorithm itself lives in the CUDA/Ascend kernel sources, which were not read.
   - *unaffected*: permutation requirement confirmed; V4.1 has Q-norm disabled
   - anchor `flash_mla/flash_mla_interface.py:L88-L92 @ 0f11e17` @ 0f11e178da1f67becb087a324b400854931ec3e3: "silently reuses stale split-KV scheduling metadata"
   - anchor `flash_mla/flash_mla_interface.py:L109-L111 @ 0f11e17` @ 0f11e178da1f67becb087a324b400854931ec3e3: "If True, the split-KV decoding path is disabled so that results do not depend on how the batch is partitioned"
4. Fused norm-RoPE-attn-RoPE-cast kernel (CUDA only) fuses Q-norm (V4 only), Q-RoPE, attention, conjugate O-RoPE and cast-to-FP8; it requires offline permutation of Q_b and Wv weights (permute_q_b_proj / permute_wv_proj) and feeds an FP8 einsum with Wv (DeepGEMM).
   - *unaffected*: backends confirmed
   - anchor `README.md §Fused kernel @ 0f11e17` @ 0f11e178da1f67becb087a324b400854931ec3e3: "at the cost of having to permute the Q_b and Wv weights in advance"
5. Backends: NVIDIA SM100/SM103 (CUDA >= 13.1) and Huawei Ascend 950; AMD Instinct is only a community pointer to ROCm aiter mla.py. Performance (vendor-reported, B200 CUDA 13.3): sparse decode up to 1024 TFLOPS, sparse prefill up to 1350, fused kernel up to 950 (decode) / 1460 (prefill); Ascend 950 decode 360 TFLOPS (83% of peak), prefill 410 (95%).
   - anchor `README.md §AMD Instinct @ 0f11e17` @ 0f11e178da1f67becb087a324b400854931ec3e3: "The corresponding FlashMLA version can be found at: [AITER/MLA](https://github.com/ROCm/aiter/blob/main/aiter/mla.py)"
6. Ascend deep-dive design facts that transfer to any device that must dequantize FP8/FP4 KV in software (our MI210 and CPU): KV dequantization to bf16 was a bottleneck even on dedicated vector cores (they pair each matrix core with two vector cores to share dequant, gather issue and softmax); token data+scale adjacency was chosen to avoid two separate copies per gathered token; "skip-scale" skips the online-softmax output rescale when the running max barely changed.
   - anchor `docs/20260930-ascend-prefill-deep-dive.md §Decoding KV Cache Layout Optimization @ 0f11e17` @ 0f11e178da1f67becb087a324b400854931ec3e3: "we place a token's data and scale next to each other, so that data and scale no longer have to be copied separately"
   - anchor `docs/20260930-ascend-prefill-deep-dive.md §Can We Use 1C1V? ¶2 @ 0f11e17` @ 0f11e178da1f67becb087a324b400854931ec3e3: "with 1C1V, dequantization cannot keep up with the CUBE Core, making dequantization the bottleneck"

### Dive corrections

- 2026-10-20 (wave 1): attn_sink/topk_length also in decode; FP4 is extra-cache only; both formats fully specified incl. scale rules; found that our fork already has the 528 B row layout and an NVFP4 type with matching scale semantic; FP8 KV type not worth adding (3% vs q8_0). 2026-10-20 second reader (independent agent): re-find 12/12; support yes 7 / partial 0 / no 0; corrections applied: none required.

### Verdict

Cite the two per-token layouts, the indices/topk_length/attn_sink contract and the scheduler-metadata reuse hazard as primary-source facts. Our fork already carries the 528 B row convention (ggml_gather_rows_e4m3_e8m0, k=16) and an NVFP4 type whose scale rule (ue4m3 of amax/6 per 16) matches the FP4 format but whose in-block byte order does not; a new FP8 KV type buys only ~3% vs q8_0 (528 vs 544 B/token), so the lever is FP4-class KV, an unmeasured quality question. Do NOT carry TFLOPS figures; do NOT claim sparse-gather speedup (C123 arithmetic is unmeasured).

## intake-2123#record — DeepGEMM: FP8/FP4 GEMM, Mega MoE fused kernel, MQA indexer logits (DeepSeek)

- **URL**: https://github.com/deepseek-ai/DeepGEMM
- **Verification**: dive-verified
- **Verdict**: adopt_patterns (novelty medium, relevance medium; categories: moe_optimization, quantization, hardware_optimization, inference_serving)
- **Disposition**: integrated
- **Expanded from**: intake-2116#record

### Claims (as verified)

0. Mega MoE = ONE persistent SM100-only kernel: dispatch warps pull tokens (+SF) from peer ranks over NVLink symmetric memory, MMA warps run L1 (gate/up) and L2 (down) as FP8xFP4 or FP8xFP8 GEMMs scheduled so L2 tiles of an M block start as soon as that block's L1 tiles finish, epilogue warps do SwiGLU+topk-weight+FP8 requant between them and write L2 output straight into peers' combine buffers, then a combine stage reduces the top-k slots. Overlap is intra-kernel warp-role pipelining, not stream overlap.
   - *unaffected*: Stage-1 fusion claim confirmed; added the mechanism (warp-role pipelining inside one launch).
   - anchor `README.md:L114 @ 5081f85` @ 5081f85: "Mega MoE fuses and overlaps EP dispatch, linear 1 and linear 2 (FP8xFP4 or FP8xFP8), SwiGLU, and EP combine into a single mega-kernel, overlapping NVLink communication and tensor core computation."
1. Masked grouped GEMM = fixed-capacity [G, max_m, K] activations plus a device int32 masked_m[G]; only valid rows are computed, so the launch shape is static (CUDA-graph safe) while per-expert counts stay on device. Contiguous layout is the dynamic-shape prefill counterpart. Both group only M.
   - *narrowed*: Masked layout made precise: static [G,max_m,K] shape + device masked_m; only M grouped; expected_m is a host tuning hint.
   - anchor `README.md:L84 @ 5081f85` @ 5081f85: "During the inference decoding phase, when CUDA graph is enabled and the CPU is unaware of the number of tokens each expert receives, we support masked grouped GEMMs."
2. MQA indexer logits: per query row i, over KV span [start_i, end_i): sum over heads of relu(q_h . dequant(kv_j)) * w_{i,h}; output compressed so each row's valid span begins at column 0 (width max_seqlen_k); q/kv may be MXFP4 or MXFP8 with packed UE8M0 scales on SM100, E4M3 with a float scale per KV token on SM90.
   - *unaffected*: Indexer-logit semantics now stated exactly (weighted ReLU over heads, per-query span, compressed output).
   - anchor `README.md:L100-L106 @ 5081f85` @ 5081f85: "For each token `i` in `q`, it will iterate all tokens `j` from `[cu_seq_len_k_start[i], cu_seq_len_k_end[i])`,"
   - anchor `README.md:L106 @ 5081f85` @ 5081f85: "out_ij = out_ij.relu() * weights[i, :] # [num_heads]"
3. Mega MoE weight layout transform (transform_weights_for_mega_moe): L1 weight rows reordered from [gate | up] to alternating blocks of 8 rows [gate0..7, up0..7, gate8..15, ...] (same for its SF) so one accumulator tile holds matching gate/up pairs and SwiGLU is a purely in-register epilogue; L1 SF additionally transposed (4x32 -> 32x4 groups) for the UTCCP SF copy; L2 weights unchanged except the SF transpose.
   - *unaffected*: New: gate/up interleave and SF transpose, not in Stage 1.
   - anchor `deep_gemm/mega/__init__.py:L113 @ 5081f85` @ 5081f85: "# [gate: 0..7, up: 0..7, gate: 8..15, up: 8..15, ...] instead of [gate | up]"
4. L1 epilogue = silu(gate)*up (optional clamp: gate<=c, -c<=up<=c; fast-math exp/rcp), multiplied by the per-token topk weight BEFORE L2, then amax/FP8-requantised for L2 input; L2 epilogue is BF16 written to the destination rank's combine slot indexed by topk slot, so the final combine is a plain sum over topk slots with no extra weighting pass.
   - *unaffected*: New: epilogue dataflow, not in Stage 1.
   - anchor `deep_gemm/include/deep_gemm/impls/sm100_fp8_fp4_mega_moe.cuh:L1066 @ 5081f85` @ 5081f85: "// Unified L1 epilogue: SwiGLU in-place using granularity 8 interleaved weights"
   - anchor `deep_gemm/include/deep_gemm/impls/sm100_fp8_fp4_mega_moe.cuh:L1273 @ 5081f85` @ 5081f85: "// L2 BF16 epilogue: write GEMM output to remote combine buffer via NVLink"
5. Operand formats: FP8 = E4M3 activations; FP4 = E2M1 packed two per byte (low nibble first) with one UE8M0 scale per 32 K-elements (recipe (1,1,32)); scales are exact powers of two (float32 with zero mantissa) and ship packed 4-per-int32, MN-major, TMA-aligned.
   - *unaffected*: New: FP4/FP8 operand and UE8M0 scale formats.
   - anchor `README.md:L68 @ 5081f85` @ 5081f85: "SM100 requires scaling factors in packed [UE8M0](https://docs.nvidia.com/cuda/parallel-thread-execution/#alternate-floating-point-data-formats) format, which packs 4 UE8M0 into a single `torch.int`."
6. UE8M0 scale rule in the reference quantisers: scale = amax / qmax (qmax 448 for E4M3, 6 for E2M1) then rounded UP to the next power of two (exponent + 1 if any mantissa bit is set, clamped to [1,254]); this differs from ggml's MXFP4 quantiser (floor(log2(amax)) - 2 + 127, ggml-quants.c:L368 @ dd3d9721c), so a re-quantised MXFP4 tensor is not bit-identical across the two recipes even though decode (value * 2^(e-127)) is the same.
   - *unaffected*: New: UE8M0 round-up differs from the ggml MXFP4 quantiser.
   - anchor `deep_gemm/utils/math.py:L15 @ 5081f85` @ 5081f85: "exp = ((bits >> 23) & 0xFF) + (bits & 0x7FFFFF).bool().int()"
7. Vendor-reported Mega MoE benchmarks (PR #316, EP8, hardware not stated in the PR body): DeepSeek-V4-Flash (256 experts, top-k 6, hidden 4096, intermediate 2048) 56.5 us at 1 token/rank (5 TFLOPS, 1311 GB/s global memory, 1.96x vs the unfused DeepEP+grouped-GEMM baseline) rising to 4855.5 us / 2038 TFLOPS at 32768 tokens/rank (1.62x). Speedup is largest at batch 1, where it is launch/latency-bound.
   - *unaffected*: Stage 1 said benchmarks unread; PR #316 body now read. Numbers carry no GPU model.
   - anchor `PR #316 body (gh api repos/deepseek-ai/DeepGEMM/pulls/316, saved pr316.json), DeepSeek-V4-Flash table` @ 5081f85: "| 1 | 56.5 | 5 | 1311 | 1 | 1.96x |"
   - anchor `PR #316 body, preamble` @ 5081f85: "We benchmarked Mega MoE on DeepSeek-V4-Flash and DeepSeek-V4-Pro under 8-way expert parallelism (EP8), testing at various batch sizes (i.e., the number of tokens per rank) to cover different serving scenarios. All values are averaged across 8 ranks."
8. Platform: Mega MoE needs SM100 (Blackwell), PyTorch >= 2.9 symmetric memory and a multi-process EP launch; no HIP/ROCm and no CPU path. The portable content is the dataflow (interleaved gate/up layout, in-epilogue SwiGLU, topk-weight folded before the down projection, static-shape masked grouping), not the code.
   - *narrowed*: Stage 1 said NVIDIA SM90/SM100; Mega MoE itself is SM100-only, masked/contiguous GEMM and MQA also exist on SM90.
   - anchor `README.md:L31 @ 5081f85` @ 5081f85: "NVIDIA SM90 or SM100 architecture GPU"
   - anchor `deep_gemm/include/deep_gemm/impls/sm100_fp8_fp4_mega_moe.cuh:L80 @ 5081f85` @ 5081f85: "#if (defined(__CUDA_ARCH__) and (__CUDA_ARCH__ >= 1000)) or defined(__CLION_IDE__)"

### Dive corrections

- 2026-10-20 (wave 1): Stage 1 listed benchmarks as unread: PR #316 body now read and recorded with the honest caveat that it names no GPU. Stage 1 said NVIDIA SM90/SM100: Mega MoE is SM100-only. Added the weight-layout, epilogue and UE8M0 conventions Stage 1 did not capture. Independently confirmed the lane finding (DS41-C66) that iqk_moe_fused_up_gate has no caller in the dd3d9721c lineage. 2026-10-20 second reader (independent agent): re-find 13/13; support yes 9 / partial 0 / no 0; corrections applied: none required.

### Verdict

Cite DeepGEMM for (a) the Mega MoE dataflow shape (interleaved gate/up layout, in-epilogue SwiGLU with the topk weight folded before the down projection, static-shape masked grouping with device counts) as a design reference, and (b) the exact MQA-indexer-logits and FP4/UE8M0 conventions as an oracle spec for DS41 indexer work. Do NOT carry the 1.5x-1.96x speedups as an expectation for any EPYC path: they are vendor-reported, EP8 over NVLink with the GPU model unstated, and the baseline is DeepEP+unfused kernels, a comm-bound situation that CPU single-socket MoE decode does not have. The fusion that does transfer (up+gate+SwiGLU in one tile loop) already exists in our tree as an unreached hook (iqk_moe_fused_up_gate) and its measured payoff is bounded by the per-node barrier share, which the lane measured as small versus the gemv itself.

### Claims (as verified)

0. Dozens of TileLang kernels (33 kernel modules; 32 CUDA SM90/SM100 and 33 Ascend 950 implementation files, backend selected at runtime by the presence of /dev/davinci_manager); the README asserts near-roofline performance with no numbers and the repo records none.
   - *unaffected*: Stage-1 claim of no numbers confirmed; module and backend file counts added.
   - anchor `README.md:L3 @ c1cf914` @ c1cf914: "Most kernels achieve performance close to the hardware's compute or memory bandwidth limits."
1. Kernel families (directory = family): engram (gate fwd/bwd, fused weight, grad-w reduce, hash, sinkhorn momentum); mhc (expand, head_compute_mix, pre_split_mixes, pre_apply_mix, post, norm_fn, pre_big_fuse, multilayer_recompute, sinkhorn); moe (topk gate fwd/bwd with sqrtsoftplus scoring, normalize_weight, sqrt_softplus helper); quant (per-token/per-block/per-channel cast, cast_back, per-block lossless, per_token_cast_and_cast_back, fused rmsnorm fwd/bwd, swiglu fwd/bwd with quant, weight-SF transpose); transform (RoPE); rand (randn); modeling (Engram autograd wrapper); torch (pure PyTorch reference per family). Dense/grouped GEMMs are in DeepGEMM, not here.
   - *narrowed*: Family list made exact; dense/grouped GEMMs are in DeepGEMM not here.
   - anchor `README.md:L14-L16 @ c1cf914` @ c1cf914: "- **Quantization** — Per-token, per-block, and per-channel FP8/FP4 casting and dequantization, with fused SwiGLU+quantization ops"
2. No HIP/ROCm/AMD path exists in the repo: two backends only (CUDA, Ascend), no third branch in config.py or any kernel wrapper. TileLang itself lists HIP as supported with gfx942-only CI and gfx90a codegen-only coverage (cited from the lane entry intake-2029#record, not re-verified here), so a gfx90a port of TileKernels would mean writing a new per-kernel HIP implementation, and fp8/fp4 would need dequant emulation (MI210 has no fp8 MFMA).
   - *unaffected*: HIP/ROCm absence settled by whole-tree grep plus config.py (two backends only).
   - anchor `README.md:L27-L30 @ c1cf914` @ c1cf914: "NVIDIA SM90 or SM100 architecture GPU"
   - anchor `tile_kernels/config.py:L31 @ c1cf914` @ c1cf914: "return 'npu' if is_ascend() else 'cuda'"
3. torch/ references are numerically the DS41 formulas but tolerance-checked, not bit-exact: tests use assert_close at 1e-4 to 8e-3. The Engram-gate torch ref (torch/engram.py engram_gate_ref) multiplies by dot.sign() whereas the fused kernel (engram_gate_fwd_cuda.py:L165) and DS41 model.py use copysign: at dot == +0.0 the ref yields gate 0.5 and the kernel 0.50025. Our graph (ggml_sgn) matches the TileKernels REF, not the kernel/model.py; the lane already documented this 2.5e-4 gate delta on a measure-zero input.
   - *narrowed*: Stage-1 reference-as-oracle idea narrowed: refs are tolerance-checked and the engram torch ref disagrees with its own fused kernel at dot == +0.0.
   - anchor `tests/engram/test_engram_gate_fwd.py:L70 @ c1cf914` @ c1cf914: "torch.testing.assert_close(out_save, out_ref, rtol=8e-3, atol=8e-3)"
   - anchor `tile_kernels/torch/engram.py:L207 @ c1cf914` @ c1cf914: "signed_sqrt = dot.abs().clamp_min(clamp_value).sqrt() * dot.sign()"
   - anchor `tile_kernels/engram/engram_gate_fwd_cuda.py:L165 @ c1cf914` @ c1cf914: "T.copysign(T.sqrt(T.clamp(T.abs(gate_score_reducer[0]), clamp_value, float('inf'))), gate_score_reducer[0])"
   - anchor `inference/model.py:L362 @ DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash local snapshot` @ c1cf914: "gate = torch.sigmoid(torch.copysign(dot.abs().clamp_min(self.clamp_value).sqrt(), dot))"
4. mHC Sinkhorn reference (torch/mhc.py sinkhorn_normalize_ref): comb = softmax(-1) + eps; comb /= (sum(-2) + eps); then (repeat-1) times: comb /= (sum(-1)+eps); comb /= (sum(-2)+eps); default repeat 10, eps 1e-6. This is op-for-op the DS41 inference/kernel.py hc_split_sinkhorn loop (V4.1 uses 20 iterations from config) and our ggml build_hc_sinkhorn (deepseek4.cpp L310-L345 @ dd3d9721c: soft_max, +eps, norm_cols, then (iters-1) x norm_rows+norm_cols). mhc_pre_split_mixes_ref differs only in taking post multiplier as a parameter (DS41 hardcodes 2 * sigmoid).
   - *unaffected*: Sinkhorn reference equals DS41 kernel.py and our fused op loop.
   - anchor `tile_kernels/torch/mhc.py:L8-L14 @ c1cf914` @ c1cf914: "x = x.softmax(-1) + eps"
   - anchor `inference/kernel.py:L436-L448 @ DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash local snapshot` @ c1cf914: "# comb = comb.softmax(-1) + eps"
   - anchor `tile_kernels/torch/mhc.py:L46 @ c1cf914` @ c1cf914: "post_layer_mix = (input_mixes[:, :, mhc_mult : 2 * mhc_mult].sigmoid() * mhc_post_mult_value).unsqueeze(-1)"
5. Quantisation conventions (torch/cast.py, config.py): E4M3 max 448, E2M1 max 6; dequant scale = amax / qmax; for UE8M0 scales the scale is rounded UP to a power of two by integer trick (bits + 0x007FFFFF) & 0x7F800000, identical to DeepGEMM's ceil_to_ue8m0; E2M1 packs two codes per int8, low nibble first (layout s|e|m); amax clamped below (1e-4 for e4m3 unscaled). Packed UE8M0 stores 4 exponents per int32. cast_back dequantises as value * repeat_interleave(scale) over (block_h, block_w) blocks, which equals DS41's Engram row dequant (fp8 values * per-block scale, model.py:L318-L319 in the local DS41 snapshot) for block (1, block_size).
   - *unaffected*: Quant conventions recorded; UE8M0 round-up identical to DeepGEMM and different from the ggml MXFP4 quantiser.
   - anchor `tile_kernels/torch/cast.py:L229 @ c1cf914` @ c1cf914: "ds_int_rounded = (ds_int + 0x007FFFFF) & 0x7F800000"
6. Fused SwiGLU reference (torch/swiglu.py swiglu_forward): optional clamp (left <= c, right in [-c, c]) then silu(left) * right then optional per-row topk weight (times routed_scaling_factor); the same clamp/weight/order as DeepGEMM's Mega MoE L1 epilogue, so the two repos are one specification of the V4 expert FFN activation.
   - *unaffected*: Fused SwiGLU reference recorded; matches DeepGEMM epilogue order.
7. MoE gate reference (torch/topk.py moe_topk_gate_forward): scores = sqrt(softplus(logits)), a per-expert bias affects ranking only, weights are gathered from the UN-biased scores, separate image bias for image tokens; this is the V4 router that DS41 model.py Gate.forward (L808-L830) implements with score_func sqrtsoftplus.
   - *unaffected*: Router reference recorded; matches DS41 model.py Gate.

### Dive corrections

- 2026-10-20 (wave 1): Stage 1 said "README lists CUDA and Ascend backends; HIP not mentioned": now settled by whole-tree grep and config.py. New finding not in Stage 1: the repo's Engram torch reference and its own fused kernel disagree at dot == +0.0, and our ggml graph agrees with the reference, not the kernel/model.py. Also: Dense GEMMs are in DeepGEMM; fused Sinkhorn op already exists in our tree (GGML_OP_DSV4_HC_COMB), so no new fused-op task is warranted. 2026-10-20 second reader (independent agent): re-find 12/12; support yes 6 / partial 0 / no 0; corrections applied: none required.

### Verdict

Cite TileKernels for (a) the existence of an independent PyTorch statement of the V4 Engram gate, mHC Sinkhorn, sqrtsoftplus router, SwiGLU+clamp and FP8/FP4/UE8M0 conventions, and (b) the fact that it has NO AMD path (CUDA + Ascend only). Do NOT carry it as a bit-level oracle: its own tests are tolerance checks and its engram torch ref differs from its fused kernel at dot == +0.0. The first-party DS41 snapshot (inference/model.py, kernel.py) remains the oracle of record for DS41-B3/B4; TileKernels refs are a cross-check only. Do NOT carry any performance expectation: the repo records no numbers.

## intake-2125#record — You Only Cache Once: Decoder-Decoder Architectures for Language Models

- **URL**: https://arxiv.org/abs/2405.05254
- **Verification**: dive-verified
- **Verdict**: worth_investigating (novelty medium, relevance medium; categories: kv_cache, inference_serving, context_extension, ssm_hybrid)
- **Disposition**: integrated
- **Expanded from**: intake-2116#record

### Claims (as verified)

0. Architecture (§2): L layers split L/2 self-decoder + L/2 cross-decoder; self-decoder output X^{L/2} is projected once (Eq. 2) to a single global K,V that every cross-decoder layer attends to with causal cross-attention. Self-decoder uses gated retention (default, recurrent state S_n, chunk-parallel prefill) or sliding-window attention (window C; C=1024 in the scaling runs). Both exist in the paper as separate variants.
   - *unaffected*: Stage-1 claim 0 (architecture) as stated; add that self-decoder is gRet OR SWA (two variants).
   - anchor `§2 ¶2 (Fig. 2 text)` @ arXiv 2405.05254v2 (raw HTML sha256-12 b2c5f9cea80f, saved s1/yoco/p.html): "YOCO is stacked with L blocks, where the first \frac{L}{2} layers are self-decoder while the rest modules are cross-decoder."
1. KV memory (§2.3, Table 2): Transformer O(L*N*D) vs YOCO O((N+L)D); the text derives O(N + C*L) cache entries: ONE global layer of N entries plus a constant C per self-decoder layer (C=window for SWA; gRet holds a fixed-size state, not KV). It is an L-fold saving only asymptotically in N; the paper's own footnote concedes the self-decoder caches are non-zero. Stage-1 'KV memory scales as O((N+L)D)' is accurate to Table 2.
   - *narrowed*: Stage-1 claim 1: formula right (Table 2); saving is asymptotic, self-decoder cache is constant not zero.
   - anchor `§2.3 ¶4 (memory)` @ arXiv 2405.05254v2 (raw HTML sha256-12 b2c5f9cea80f, saved s1/yoco/p.html): "For long sequences, CL is much smaller than N, so about \mathcal{O}(N) caches are required, i.e., you only cache once."
   - anchor `§1 fn.1` @ arXiv 2405.05254v2 (raw HTML sha256-12 b2c5f9cea80f, saved s1/yoco/p.html): "Strictly, self-decoder also needs to store a certain number of caches. As the self-decoder utilizes an efficient attention module, the cache size is bounded to a constant, which can be ignored compared to global caches when the sequence length is large."
2. Exact prefill early exit (§2.3, Table 1): cross-decoder layers consume only (a) their own per-position residual stream and (b) the global K,V computed from X^{L/2}; with causal masking and no cross-decoder-layer KV of their own, nothing a cross-decoder layer computes at a prompt position is ever read again by a later position or by decode. So the cross-decoder can be skipped for every prompt position whose output logits are not requested. The paper states this ('without changing the final output') but gives no proof and does not address the last-prompt-token / logits-requested case (that token must still traverse the cross-decoder to emit token 1: our inference, not in the paper). The argument is EXACT only because cross-decoder layers have no per-layer KV; it fails the moment any cross-decoder layer keeps its own KV (e.g., per-layer SWA).
   - *narrowed*: Stage-1 claim 2: exact only when cross layers have no own KV and for non-logit rows; no proof in paper. Short-context halving is a floor ("at least 2x"), 2.87x at 32K.
   - anchor `§2.3 ¶5 (prefill)` @ arXiv 2405.05254v2 (raw HTML sha256-12 b2c5f9cea80f, saved s1/yoco/p.html): "because the cross-decoder reuses the outputs of self-decoder, we can exit early before entering the cross-decoder during the prefill stage."
3. Prefill/throughput numbers are author-reported on H100-80GB, 3B YOCO-gRet (Triton kernel, chunk 256) vs a GQA+Flash-Decoding Transformer: >=2x at any length by construction (half the layers), 2.87x at 32K, 180 s -> <6 s at 512K, 71.8x at 1M (intro), 9.6x throughput at 512K (4.5 -> 43.1 tok/s), 12.4 GB total inference memory at 1M (Transformer 9.4x). Only YOCO-gRet was profiled; beyond the 2x early-exit floor the gains are driven by the linear-time gRet self-decoder replacing quadratic attention, so they do NOT transfer to a YOCO with full-attention or SWA-window-large self-decoder. Intro says 'early exit before entering the self-decoder' (typo; section 2.3 and 4.4 say cross-decoder).
   - anchor `§4.4 Prefilling Latency` @ arXiv 2405.05254v2 (raw HTML sha256-12 b2c5f9cea80f, saved s1/yoco/p.html): "As described in Section 2.3, the prefill stage can early exit before entering cross-decoder. So, there is at least two times speedup of prefilling latency even for short context."
   - anchor `§4.4 Prefilling Latency` @ arXiv 2405.05254v2 (raw HTML sha256-12 b2c5f9cea80f, saved s1/yoco/p.html): "Figure 8 shows that YOCO reduces the Transformer prefilling time from 180 seconds to less than 6 seconds for 512K context."
   - anchor `§4.4 setup` @ arXiv 2405.05254v2 (raw HTML sha256-12 b2c5f9cea80f, saved s1/yoco/p.html): "We compare YOCO{}_{\text{gRet}} with Transformer."
4. Quality (§4.1, 4.2, App. G): 3B/1.6T-token model avg 0.636 on 8 LM-Harness tasks vs OpenLLaMA-v2-3B 0.619 and StableLM-alpha-v2 0.612 at 1T (YOCO 0.634 at 1T); StableLM-3B-4E1T avg not reported (dashes). Scaling curves 160M-13B at 10B tokens: YOCO-gRet matches/beats the Llama Transformer, YOCO-SWA comparable; at 160M (Table 9) valid ppl gRet 3.530, SWA 3.553, Transformer 3.564. Differences are small, single-seed, no error bars; the gRet win is attributed to attention+retention hybridity, not to KV sharing.
   - *narrowed*: Stage-1 claim 3 (competitive 3B, 1M): averages fair but small single-seed deltas; independent YOCO++ finds a plain-YOCO quality gap; multi-needle only at 128K and mixed.
   - anchor `§4.2 Results` @ arXiv 2405.05254v2 (raw HTML sha256-12 b2c5f9cea80f, saved s1/yoco/p.html): "YOCO obtains comparable performance from 160M to 13B compared to the Llama-optimized transformer architecture."
   - anchor `§4.2 Results` @ arXiv 2405.05254v2 (raw HTML sha256-12 b2c5f9cea80f, saved s1/yoco/p.html): "The gains come from hybrid architectures of attention and retention, whose inductive biases tend to be complementary to each other."
5. Long context (§4.3): YOCO-3B-1M (progressive 64K->256K->1M, 11.5B tokens, no long-instruction data) near-perfect single-needle NIAH to 1M (Fig. 4, 10 runs per depth/length, image not machine-read); multi-needle at 128K (Table 5): N=1/2/4/8 = 0.98/0.98/0.84/0.56 vs LWM-1M-text-7B 1.00/0.90/0.76/0.62, MiniCPM-128K 1.00/1.00/0.54/0.56, ChatGLM3-128K 0.94/0.72/0.52/0.44. The paper's 'outperforms' is overstated: YOCO is below LWM at N=8 and below MiniCPM at N=1/2. Needle retrieval is a single-hop lookup; no multi-hop/reasoning long-context benchmark in the paper.
   - anchor `§4.3 Needle` @ arXiv 2405.05254v2 (raw HTML sha256-12 b2c5f9cea80f, saved s1/yoco/p.html): "Figure 4 shows that YOCO-3B-1M passes the Needle-In-A-Haystack test with near perfect accuracy."
   - anchor `§4.3 Table 5 caption` @ arXiv 2405.05254v2 (raw HTML sha256-12 b2c5f9cea80f, saved s1/yoco/p.html): "The evaluation is conducted in 128K length, because most previous long-context models are tuned with this length."
6. Requires pretraining: every YOCO model in the paper (160M-13B scaling, 3B/1.6T, 1M extension) is trained from scratch; length extension continues YOCO itself. The paper NEVER states that retrofitting a Transformer is impossible or tried (NOT-FOUND-IN-SOURCE for an explicit statement); the architectural reason is that the cross-decoder's K,V are new projections of a mid-stack representation. Treat 'cannot be retrofitted' as an inference, not a paper claim.
   - *reattributed*: Stage-1 claim 4 (cannot be retrofitted): not stated in paper; inference from from-scratch training only.
   - anchor `§4.1 ¶1` @ arXiv 2405.05254v2 (raw HTML sha256-12 b2c5f9cea80f, saved s1/yoco/p.html): "We train a 3B-size YOCO language models by scaling up the number of training tokens."
7. Independent evidence (YOCO++, arXiv 2604.13556, resolved; 1.1B TinyLlama-config, 100B SlimPajama tokens from scratch, H20): reproduces the ~half prefill latency from early exit, and finds YOCO UNDERPERFORMS the standard Transformer on training loss and downstream accuracy, attributing it to KV sharing weakening model capacity. This is a different (full-attention-bottom-half, 50%-KV-compression) YOCO variant at small scale, not the gRet 3B model, so it narrows rather than refutes the original quality claim. YOCO++ also notes YOCO caches the bottom-half layers' KVs (50% compression), not 1/L.
   - *reattributed*: Stage-1 contradicting_evidence: YOCO++ id 2604.13556 resolved; claims capacity loss and measures a quality gap, NOT complex-reasoning harm.
   - anchor `YOCO++ §4.2 ¶4` @ arXiv 2604.13556 (HTML on disk dive-deepinfer-yoco/yocopp.html): "In terms of both training loss and downstream task accuracy, YOCO underperforms the standard Transformer, while FusedKV-Lite, FusedKV and YOCO++ outperforms the standard Transformer."
   - anchor `YOCO++ §1 ¶3` @ arXiv 2604.13556 (HTML on disk dive-deepinfer-yoco/yocopp.html): "The main cause of this degradation is that the direct KV sharing from one layer to another weakens the model capacity."
8. SambaY (arXiv 2507.06607, resolved; Microsoft-affiliated, thanks the YOCO authors, so semi-independent): trains its own Samba+YOCO and SWA+YOCO baselines and reports SambaY with significantly lower irreducible loss than Samba+YOCO; it re-describes YOCO's self-decoder as ending in a full-attention layer that produces the KV (YOCO paper's Eq. 2 projects from X^{L/2} instead), and says a Transformer++ is expressively equivalent in the limit. It restates YOCO's linear prefill; it does not measure a YOCO capacity cost directly.
   - *unaffected*: Stage-1 contradicting_evidence: SambaY 2507.06607 resolved; lower irreducible loss vs self-trained Samba+YOCO.
   - anchor `SambaY §1` @ arXiv 2507.06607 (HTML on disk dive-deepinfer-yoco/sambay.html): "Comparing to Samba+YOCO, an architecture that naively combines Samba with YOCO, we show that SambaY has significantly lower irreducible loss [HNA+17] on the validation set when scaling with the training FLOPs, indicating a better scaling potential with large-scale computes."

### Dive corrections

- 2026-10-20 (wave 1): Stage-1 'roughly halves prefill' narrowed to 'at least 2x by construction, bigger only for gRet on H100'; exactness of early exit qualified (no logits-requested case, requires KV-less cross layers); YOCO++ reattributed from 'complex reasoning' to 'model capacity / quality gap vs Transformer' with the id and title resolved; SambaY title resolved; 'cannot be retrofitted' downgraded from claim to inference; Stage-1 'depends on intake-2117#record' not an evidential dependency for YOCO itself. 2026-10-20 second reader (independent agent): re-find 15/15; support yes 3 / partial 6 / no 0. All 6 partials arose because the reader was not given the YOCO paper text path (only YOCO++/SambaY); main spot-checked the disputed numbers (71.8, 2.87, 9.6, 0.636, 3.530, 12.4) present in the saved paper text /mnt/raid0/llm/tmp/dive-deepinfer-main/s1/yoco/p.txt. Remaining disagreement: none of substance; anchors for those numbers are thin (carried to Stage 3 as anchor gaps). 2026-10-20 STAGE-2 CLOSE-OUT: dive-surfaced arXiv 2507.06607 (SambaY / Decoder-Hybrid-Decoder) DECLINED per the dive recommendation: no served checkpoint of this family; its YOCO-relevant content is recorded in this entry's claims. Carried to the Stage-3 plan for operator confirmation.

### Verdict

Cite YOCO for two things only: the O(N+CL) cache formula and the exactness condition for prefill early exit (layers with no own KV that read only a shared KV can be skipped for non-logit prompt rows). Do NOT carry its 71.8x/9.6x/80x numbers (3B gRet, H100, author-run) or 'outperforms Transformer' (independent YOCO++ finds a plain-YOCO capacity gap at 1.1B). DS41's decoder-SWA bounded replay is NOT the YOCO argument: it is an approximation needed because every DS41 decoder layer keeps its own SWA KV. Retrofit-impossibility is an inference.

### Linked handoffs and actions

- updated `deepseek-v41-flash-evaluation.md`
- updated `kv-unified-stack-rollout.md`
- Recommended action (dive proposal, not an instruction): Operator-review candidate: use YOCO's exact prefill early-exit argument to separate what is exact from what is approximate in the DS41 CED prefill truncation.

## intake-2126#record — NanoFlow: Towards Optimal Large Language Model Serving Throughput

- **URL**: https://arxiv.org/abs/2408.12757
- **Verification**: dive-verified
- **Verdict**: adopt_patterns (novelty medium, relevance medium; categories: inference_serving, hardware_optimization, kv_cache)
- **Disposition**: integrated
- **Expanded from**: intake-2116#record

### Claims (as verified)

0. COST MODEL: a closed-form comparison of memory time (MemSize/MemBW), compute time (2 x B_dense x P_model / Compute) and network time argues batched serving is compute-bound for common workloads: ratio T_mem/T_compute < 1 for LLaMA-2-70B (8xA100, B_dense up to ~2048 given GQA), LLaMA-3-70B, Qwen2-72B, Mixtral 8x7B, with the stated exception of LLaMA-3-8B on 1xA100 at long decode (512-1024) where the ratio is ~1. Optimal throughput = Compute/(2 P_model) = 1857 tok/s/GPU for LLaMA-2-70B on A100. Validated on LLaMA-2-70B with dense batch 2048 (Table 2): KQV/O/UG/D GEMMs compute-bound, decode attention memory-bound.
   - *narrowed*: Compute-bound regime requires dense batches of ~2k tokens; validated on one 8xA100 config.
   - anchor `Section 3.3` @ arXiv 2408.12757v2, read from s1/nanoflow/p.txt: "except in the case of the long decode (512-1024) workload on LLaMA-3 8B model"
   - anchor `Section 3.5 Eq. 5` @ arXiv 2408.12757v2, read from s1/nanoflow/p.txt: "Optimal throughput (measured in token/s/GPU) is achieved when the most limited resource, i.e. compute, is fully utilized."
   - anchor `Section 6.2` @ arXiv 2408.12757v2, read from s1/nanoflow/p.txt: "Since the optimal throughput is independent of user query statistics, for all the experiments, the optimal throughput is 1857 1857 tokens/s/GPU as computed in § 3.5 ."
1. PRECONDITION for nano-batching: it duplicates every operation per nano-batch and so reloads weights repeatedly; it pays only because the whole workload is compute-bound by a factor > 2, so extra weight traffic hides under compute. Splitting into nano-batches WITHOUT overlap costs 13.2%. NanoFlow assumes an abundance of requests (dense token batch of thousands, request batch of hundreds) and tells the control plane to shrink instance count when requests are scarce.
   - *narrowed*: Isolated overlap gains are 1.07x and 1.17x; nano-batch splitting alone is -13.2%.
   - anchor `Section 3.7` @ arXiv 2408.12757v2, read from s1/nanoflow/p.txt: "Since modern models are largely compute-bound (by a factor of more than 2 as shown in Figure 3 and Table 2 ), creating and processing smaller batches may be justified, especially if compute utilization can be increased."
   - anchor `Section 1` @ arXiv 2408.12757v2, read from s1/nanoflow/p.txt: "However, when the workload is compute-bound as a whole, the additional memory I/O can be hidden via pipelining."
   - anchor `Section 4.2.1` @ arXiv 2408.12757v2, read from s1/nanoflow/p.txt: "A NanoFlow instance assumes an abundance of requests and treats each request with equal priority."
   - anchor `Section 4.2.1` @ arXiv 2408.12757v2, read from s1/nanoflow/p.txt: "When requests are not abundant, the control plane should reduce the number of NanoFlow instances to maintain a sufficiently large per-instance batch size."
   - anchor `Section 6.4` @ arXiv 2408.12757v2, read from s1/nanoflow/p.txt: "As shown in Figure 9 , splitting into nano-batches alone reduces performance by 13.2 13.2 %."
2. OVERLAP GAINS (ablation, same async scheduler and kernels in all arms, 8xA100 TP=8): 1.07x from overlapping network-bound with compute-bound kernels (prefill-only, input 512), 1.17x from overlapping network+memory-bound with compute (input 512, output 1024), versus a non-overlapped baseline. Kernel interference keeps average compute utilization at 68.5%, below optimal. Pipeline found by a two-stage MILP-based auto-search with profiled kernel interference.
   - *unaffected*: Confirmed. EOS cost <1% hinges on avg decode >100; one extra STEP, which under spec decode is a full verify step.
   - anchor `Section 6.4` @ arXiv 2408.12757v2, read from s1/nanoflow/p.txt: "NanoFlow achieves 1.07 × 1.07\times speedup by overlapping network-bound kernels and 1.17 × 1.17\times speedup by overlapping both network- and memory- bound kernels compared with the non-overlapped baseline."
   - anchor `Section 6.5` @ arXiv 2408.12757v2, read from s1/nanoflow/p.txt: "Due to kernel interference, NanoFlow provides lower than optimal compute usage."
3. ASYNC BATCH FORMATION: the CPU scheduler (memory estimation, admitting new requests, retiring finished ones, page-table updates for PagedAttention) is non-trivial CPU time; NanoFlow forms the batch for iteration i+1 before iteration i ends, so it cannot see EOS tokens produced in iteration i. After launching i+1 it forms i+2 and removes the finished requests then. Cost: one extra decode step per finished request, claimed < 1% because average decode length exceeds 100 tokens (dataset output means 211-322).
   - *unaffected*: Confirmed; overhead 3.0%.
   - anchor `Section 4.2.1 Asynchronous scheduling` @ arXiv 2408.12757v2, read from s1/nanoflow/p.txt: "consumes a non-negligible amount of time on the CPU side"
   - anchor `Section 4.2.1 Asynchronous scheduling` @ arXiv 2408.12757v2, read from s1/nanoflow/p.txt: "At any iteration i i , NanoFlow forms the batch for the next iteration before the end of the current iteration."
   - anchor `Section 4.2.1 Asynchronous scheduling` @ arXiv 2408.12757v2, read from s1/nanoflow/p.txt: "the overhead of one extra decode token is negligible"
   - anchor `Table 4` @ arXiv 2408.12757v2, read from s1/nanoflow/p.txt: "Dataset Avg. Input (Std) Avg. Output (Std) Splitwise [ 32 ] 1155 (1109) 211 (163) LMSYS-Chat [ 56 ] 102 (169) 222 (210) ShareGPT [ 1 ] 246 (547) 322 (244)"
   - anchor `tools/server/server-context.cpp:L4453 + L4460 @ dd3d9721c` @ dd3d9721c:tools/server/server-context.cpp: "// here we have synchronized the llama_context (due to the sampling above), so we can do time measurement"
4. PER-LAYER KV OFFLOAD: instead of waiting for completion, the KV of new tokens is copied to host memory right after KQV generation in each layer, before being appended to the KV cache, so host and GPU hold the same copy of running requests; the device-to-host copy is a GPU-initiated copy using few resources, issued during compute-bound FFN ops, with NUMA-aware host thread binding; LRU hierarchy host RAM then SSD; reload via a contiguous staging buffer then scatter gives 7-10x higher H2D bandwidth than copying to fragmented pages. Overhead 3.0% of pipeline throughput; saves 3.02x compute on multi-round LMSYS-Chat.
   - *narrowed*: 1.91x is vs TensorRT-LLM on dataset lengths; vs vLLM 4.18x; not an average of the three.
   - anchor `Section 4.2.2` @ arXiv 2408.12757v2, read from s1/nanoflow/p.txt: "Instead of waiting for requests’ completion, NanoFlow offloads the KV-cache of tokens directly after the KQV generation in each transformer layer before appending them to the KV-cache."
   - anchor `Section 4.2.2` @ arXiv 2408.12757v2, read from s1/nanoflow/p.txt: "NanoFlow performs the device-host copy—a GPU-initiated copy operation that uses minor GPU resources—during compute-bound operations in FFN."
   - anchor `Section 6.4` @ arXiv 2408.12757v2, read from s1/nanoflow/p.txt: "Enabling offloading would slow down the pipeline by"
5. THROUGHPUT CLAIMS WITH CONDITIONS (vendor-reported, UW + OSDI 2025, not independently reproduced): LLaMA-2-70B on 8xA100-80GB NVLink, TP=8, FP16, dense batch 2048, offline: with lengths drawn from datasets (Splitwise/LMSYS/ShareGPT) 4.18x vs vLLM 0.5.3, 3.45x vs DeepSpeed-FastGen 0.2.3, 1.91x vs TensorRT-LLM 0.8.0 (the headline 1.91x is the TensorRT-LLM figure, not an average over all three); constant lengths 2.62x / 2.78x / 1.73x; 68.5% of the derived optimum in the best case; other models 50-72% of optimum (2.66x vs vLLM, constant 1024 in / 512 out); online: 1.64x higher request rate than TensorRT-LLM within a 200 ms normalized-latency SLO on LMSYS-Chat, slightly higher latency at low rates. Decomposition of the 1.91x between async scheduling, constant dense batch, custom kernels and overlap is NOT given: the only isolated overlap gains are 1.07x and 1.17x.
   - anchor `Section 6.2` @ arXiv 2408.12757v2, read from s1/nanoflow/p.txt: "When input and output lengths are drawn from the datasets, NanoFlow has on average 4.18 × 4.18\times higher throughput than vLLM, 3.45 × 3.45\times higher than DeepSpeed-FastGen and 1.91 × 1.91\times higher than TensorRT-LLM."
   - anchor `Section 6.2` @ arXiv 2408.12757v2, read from s1/nanoflow/p.txt: "NanoFlow has the highest throughput in all settings and is able to achieve 68.5 68.5 % of the theoretical optimal throughput in the best case."
   - anchor `Section 6.6` @ arXiv 2408.12757v2, read from s1/nanoflow/p.txt: "All evaluations are done on 8 × 8\times A100 80GB SXM GPUs, except for LLaMA-3-8B, which runs on a single A100 80GB SXM."

### Dive corrections

- 2026-10-20 (wave 1): Stage 1 had not read Section 6.3-6.6. Now: 1.91x is the dataset-length gain vs TensorRT-LLM (vLLM 4.18x), not an average over all three; isolated overlap gain is 1.07-1.17x and nano-batch splitting alone costs 13.2%; async scheduling is not ablated; champion llama-server verified sequential (no async batch formation), so the Stage-1 candidate action (measure host gap) stands and is now specified with existing DEBUG_TIMINGS instrumentation. 2026-10-20 second reader (independent agent, two passes): re-find 21/21; support yes 6 / partial 0 / no 0 on the v2 pass. A first strict pass flagged claim 2's '68.5% average compute utilization' as a conflation with '68.5% of optimal throughput'; main checked the saved paper text: §6.5 states both ('achieves 68.5% average compute utilization'), so the claim stands; corrections applied: none.

### Verdict

Cite NanoFlow for (a) the exact async-batch-formation contract (batch i+1 formed before i completes, EOS from i seen after launching i+1, cost one extra step, <1% only because mean decode > 100), (b) the per-layer write-through KV offload design and its measured 3.0% cost, (c) the regime test T_mem/T_compute. Do NOT cite the headline as 1.91x over all baselines (it is vs TensorRT-LLM; vLLM is 4.18x) and do NOT treat nano-batch overlap as the source of the gain: isolated overlap is 1.07-1.17x and splitting alone is -13.2%. Nano-batching does not transfer to our bandwidth-bound CPU decode or np4 MI210 decode; llama-server on the champion has no async batch formation (sequential loop with a sync at sampling), and its measured host gap is unknown.

### Linked handoffs and actions

- updated `gpu-serving-tie-in-program.md`
- updated `kv-unified-stack-rollout.md`
- updated `heterogeneous-slot-fabric-residency.md`
- Recommended action (dive proposal, not an instruction): Operator-review candidate: measure the inter-step host gap of llama-server on the MI210 before considering asynchronous batch formation.

## intake-2127#record — PowerAttention: Exponentially Scaling of Receptive Fields for Effective Sparse Attention

- **URL**: https://arxiv.org/abs/2503.03588
- **Verification**: dive-verified
- **Verdict**: worth_investigating (novelty medium, relevance medium; categories: kv_cache, context_extension, inference_serving)
- **Disposition**: integrated
- **Expanded from**: intake-2117#record

### Claims (as verified)

0. PowerAttention defines the receptive field as the set of tokens that can influence a tokens representation and models stacked sparse-attention layers as a DAG; for a sliding window of width W the multi-layer receptive field grows only linearly with depth (O(N) layers to reach distance N), which is the upper bound that n_win x L arithmetic assumes.
   - *unaffected*: definition and linear-growth statement are verbatim.
   - anchor `§2 Static Sparse Attention ¶2` @ arXiv 2503.03588v1 (5 Mar 2025); HTML read from disk, file sha256-12 da4291a6b476; only version v1 exists: "This inherent locality implies that the range of context that sliding window attention can capture (which we refer to as receptive field later) expands linearly with the number of layers."
   - anchor `§3.1 ¶1` @ arXiv 2503.03588v1 (5 Mar 2025); HTML read from disk, file sha256-12 da4291a6b476; only version v1 exists: "We define the receptive field as the set of all tokens that can influence a given token’s representation."
1. Measured on Qwen2-7B (28 layers, 32K context, 256-token blocks) with a synthetic passkey task, achieved retrieval accuracy by passkey position tracks the THEORETICAL reachable-block fraction for sliding window as well as for PowerAttention (Fig. 1b; only one worked number, 10/128 = 7.82% for one layer, is in the text; the curves are not recoverable from text).
   - *narrowed*: figure-only data; text gives one worked number
   - anchor `Fig. 1 caption` @ arXiv 2503.03588v1 (5 Mar 2025); HTML read from disk, file sha256-12 da4291a6b476; only version v1 exists: "The actual token retrieval capability closely matches the theoretical receptive field growth for both patterns."
2. The papers own statement closest to the V4.1 reports claim: a sliding window of roughly 2K tokens retrieves passkeys up to about 12K tokens and fails beyond 16K, and the authors estimate information strength decays to near zero after propagating through 6 layers of a 28-layer model, i.e. the usable depth is about 6 of 28 layers (an estimate, not a controlled measurement of effective receptive field).
   - *narrowed*: the 6-of-28 figure is an authors estimate on a retrofitted 2K-window model
   - anchor `§4.3 ¶3` @ arXiv 2503.03588v1 (5 Mar 2025); HTML read from disk, file sha256-12 da4291a6b476; only version v1 exists: "With a window size of approximately 2K tokens, we estimate that information strength decays to near zero after propagating through 6 layers."
   - anchor `§4.3 ¶3` @ arXiv 2503.03588v1 (5 Mar 2025); HTML read from disk, file sha256-12 da4291a6b476; only version v1 exists: "sliding window performs well up to 12K tokens but degrades significantly at 16K and 32K lengths"
3. Probing (logistic classifiers on pooled hidden states, N=1200, 16K context, 6 passkeys, chance 1/6): in the unmodified full-attention model information still spreads layer by layer with spatial locality; the sliding-window last-token probe accuracy is 0.48 before and 0.37 after post-training, so the model does not learn to push passkey information further through a window stack.
   - *unaffected*: App. C.2 verbatim
   - anchor `Appendix C.2` @ arXiv 2503.03588v1 (5 Mar 2025); HTML read from disk, file sha256-12 da4291a6b476; only version v1 exists: "Interestingly, the model’s performance in information flow degrades post-training, with accuracy declining from 0.48 to 0.37 ."
4. RULER (Qwen2-7B hybrid, 2 full-attention layers per 7, sparse layers at up to 94% sparsity, continued pretraining on 1B SlimPajama tokens plus ChatQA2 finetune): sliding window averages 72.20/58.17/49.40/34.91 at 4k/8k/16k/32k versus full attention 91.77/86.34/84.27/79.24; variable-tracing at 32K is 10.60 (sliding window) versus 84.60 (full), the steepest collapse of any sub-task.
   - *unaffected*: Table 2 values
   - anchor `§4.4 ¶1` @ arXiv 2503.03588v1 (5 Mar 2025); HTML read from disk, file sha256-12 da4291a6b476; only version v1 exists: "we keep 2 full attention layers for every 7 layers"
   - anchor `§4.4 ¶3` @ arXiv 2503.03588v1 (5 Mar 2025); HTML read from disk, file sha256-12 da4291a6b476; only version v1 exists: "Notably, the widely adopted sliding window pattern achieves the lowest accuracy."
5. PowerAttention (power-of-2 distance slashes) beats sliding window on RULER 32K, 50.74 vs 34.91 average, and is reported 3.0x faster than full attention at 128K prefill on one A800; author-run, one base model, no MoE.
   - *unaffected*: vendor-reported
   - anchor `§4.5 End-to-end Latency` @ arXiv 2503.03588v1 (5 Mar 2025); HTML read from disk, file sha256-12 da4291a6b476; only version v1 exists: "At a context length of 128K, PowerAttention delivers speedups of 3.0 × and 1.3 × over full attention and MInference in the prefilling phase, respectively."
6. Scope limits: all evidence is a dense Qwen2-7B that was pretrained with FULL attention and retrofitted to sparse patterns with 1B-token continued pretraining; windows are ~2K tokens, not 128; no MoE model, no model natively trained with sliding-window layers, and no model that also has a compressed/global attention path. The paper never tests prefill-with-skipped-layers or any truncated-replay scheme.
   - *unaffected*: scope statement from §4.1
   - anchor `§4.1 Implementation` @ arXiv 2503.03588v1 (5 Mar 2025); HTML read from disk, file sha256-12 da4291a6b476; only version v1 exists: "We use Qwen2-7B ( Yang et al., 2024a ) , a pretrained model with 32K context length, as our base model."

### Dive corrections

- 2026-10-20 (wave 2, Stage-2b): ingested and dived in one pass; settled that the paper does not itself state or measure "much smaller than n_win x L" (it reports linear theoretical growth matched by retrieval, and a ~6-of-28 layer usable-depth estimate), and that nothing transfers directly to DS41 (window 128, 20 layers, MoE, compressed global path). 2026-10-20 second reader (independent agent): re-find 10/10; support yes 7 / partial 0 / no 0; corrections applied: none required.

### Verdict

Cite only as: a retrofitted dense 7B with ~2K windows shows usable propagation of roughly 6 of 28 layers (authors estimate) and sliding-window stacks collapse on multi-hop tasks (RULER VT). Do NOT cite it as having measured "effective receptive field << n_win x L" nor as evidence about a 128-token window, 20 decoder layers, MoE, or a model with compressed global attention. It does not justify a 134-140 token replay; if anything it says information crosses several window hops, so the DS41 replay must be gated empirically on long-range multi-hop tasks.

### Linked handoffs and actions

- updated `deepseek-v41-flash-evaluation.md`
- Recommended action (dive proposal, not an instruction): Operator-review candidate: treat as a prior only for the DS41-CED-1 / DS41-YOCO-1 quality gate; the gate must include multi-hop long-range tasks and context lengths well above the replay window.

## intake-2128#record — YOCO++: Enhancing YOCO with KV Residual Connections for Efficient LLM Inference

- **URL**: https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.13556
- **Verification**: dive-verified
- **Verdict**: adopt_patterns (novelty medium, relevance medium; categories: kv_cache, inference_serving, local_inference)
- **Disposition**: integrated
- **Expanded from**: intake-2125#record

### Claims (as verified)

0. YOCO splits an M-layer decoder into a self-decoder (bottom half, whose middle layer produces the one global KV cache) and a cross-decoder (top half, which reuses that cache and stores none); the paper states this halves KV cache and, because the top half can be skipped, halves prefill latency via early exit.
   - *unaffected*: §3.1 verbatim
   - anchor `§3.1 ¶1` @ arXiv 2604.13556v1 (15 Apr 2026); HTML read from disk (yocopp.html sha256-12 e7234e578ad2); only version v1: "which reuses the global KV cache and does not generate KV cache by itself."
   - anchor `§3.1 ¶1` @ arXiv 2604.13556v1 (15 Apr 2026); HTML read from disk (yocopp.html sha256-12 e7234e578ad2); only version v1: "The prefilling latency is also reduced by 50% because we can exit early before computing the top-half layers."
1. Capacity-loss evidence against plain YOCO: 1.1B TinyLlama-config models (22 layers, 32 heads, 4 KV heads), 100B SlimPajama tokens from scratch, standard attention in the self-decoder. Zero-shot average over six commonsense tasks: Transformer 48.37, YOCO 47.98 (-0.39), YOCO++ 48.99 (+0.62 vs Transformer). Worst YOCO task delta is WinoGrande 53.59 vs 56.04. Single training run, no seeds or error bars.
   - *narrowed*: Stage-1 relayed plain YOCO underperforms; confirmed but the gap is 0.39 avg points / 0.0106 loss, single run
   - anchor `§1 ¶2` @ arXiv 2604.13556v1 (15 Apr 2026); HTML read from disk (yocopp.html sha256-12 e7234e578ad2); only version v1: "The main cause of this degradation is that the direct KV sharing from one layer to another weakens the model capacity."
   - anchor `§4.2 ¶1` @ arXiv 2604.13556v1 (15 Apr 2026); HTML read from disk (yocopp.html sha256-12 e7234e578ad2); only version v1: "We train all the models on a 100B subset of the SlimPajama dataset (Soboleva et al., 2023) from scratch."
   - anchor `§4 ¶1` @ arXiv 2604.13556v1 (15 Apr 2026); HTML read from disk (yocopp.html sha256-12 e7234e578ad2); only version v1: "The models have 22 layers, 32 attention heads and 4 KV heads."
   - anchor `§4.2 ¶3` @ arXiv 2604.13556v1 (15 Apr 2026); HTML read from disk (yocopp.html sha256-12 e7234e578ad2); only version v1: "In terms of both training loss and downstream task accuracy, YOCO underperforms the standard Transformer"
   - anchor `§3.1 ¶1` @ arXiv 2604.13556v1 (15 Apr 2026); HTML read from disk (yocopp.html sha256-12 e7234e578ad2); only version v1: "The original YOCO uses efficient attention in the self-decoder to achieve higher efficiency, while we use standard attention in this work for fair comparison."
