   - anchor `Table 1` @ arXiv 2604.13556v1 (15 Apr 2026); HTML read from disk (yocopp.html sha256-12 e7234e578ad2); only version v1: "Transformer 51.25 33.0 56.04 27.56 52.1 70.29 48.37 YOCO 51.21 32.0 53.59 29.01 51.43 70.62 47.98"
   - anchor `Table 1` @ arXiv 2604.13556v1 (15 Apr 2026); HTML read from disk (yocopp.html sha256-12 e7234e578ad2); only version v1: "YOCO++ 52.30 34.0 55.48 27.90 53.11 71.16 48.99"
2. Validation loss ablation (10B-token runs): Transformer 2.4266, YOCO 2.4372, FusedKV-Lite 2.4258, FusedKV 2.4204, YOCO++ 2.4194; without the scaling factor YOCO++ is 2.4333 (about YOCO), without key residual 2.4264. The YOCO-to-Transformer gap is 0.0106 nats.
   - *unaffected*: Table 2
   - anchor `§4.3 ¶1` @ arXiv 2604.13556v1 (15 Apr 2026); HTML read from disk (yocopp.html sha256-12 e7234e578ad2); only version v1: "The validation loss of YOCO++ without the scaling factor"
   - anchor `Table 2` @ arXiv 2604.13556v1 (15 Apr 2026); HTML read from disk (yocopp.html sha256-12 e7234e578ad2); only version v1: "Transformer 2.4266 YOCO 2.4372 FusedKV-Lite 2.4258 FusedKV 2.4204 YOCO++ 2.4194 w/o scaling 2.4333 w/o key residual 2.4264"
3. KV residual fix: in each bottom-half layer the cached K and V are a learned weighted combination of that layers own K/V and the bottom layers K/V, so the middle layers shared KV carries more information; weights are initialised to reproduce YOCO and a fixed scaling factor lambda (35 gave best results) is needed to make the weights learnable. It adds four scalars per layer.
   - *unaffected*: method; lambda=35 from HTML annotation
   - anchor `§3.2 ¶1` @ arXiv 2604.13556v1 (15 Apr 2026); HTML read from disk (yocopp.html sha256-12 e7234e578ad2); only version v1: "we add only the KVs of the bottom layer to those of each layer."
   - anchor `§3.2 ¶4` @ arXiv 2604.13556v1 (15 Apr 2026); HTML read from disk (yocopp.html sha256-12 e7234e578ad2); only version v1: "We initialize the residual weights by"
4. Efficiency (NVIDIA H20 96GB, HF Transformers + FlashAttention-2): YOCO, FusedKV, FusedKV-Lite and YOCO++ show almost the same prefill latency (TTFT), half of the standard Transformer, through early exit; decode throughput is higher than the Transformer via larger batch sizes; FusedKV loses the throughput advantage due to cache I/O. Latency values appear only in Figure 3, not in text.
   - *unaffected*: text states; values in figure only
   - anchor `§4.1 ¶1` @ arXiv 2604.13556v1 (15 Apr 2026); HTML read from disk (yocopp.html sha256-12 e7234e578ad2); only version v1: "which is half that of the standard Transformer, because they can exit early before computing the top-half layers."
   - anchor `§4.1 ¶2` @ arXiv 2604.13556v1 (15 Apr 2026); HTML read from disk (yocopp.html sha256-12 e7234e578ad2); only version v1: "its throughput degrades to the same level as the standard Transformer because it introduces additional I/O overhead."
5. Not tested: any long-context or retrieval task, any pretrained-model retrofit, MoE, quality at long prefill, or accuracy degradation attributable to early exit itself. YOCO++ is a training-time architecture change; it cannot be applied to an existing checkpoint.
   - *unaffected*: absence statements verified by reading whole §4

### Dive corrections

- 2026-10-20 (wave 2, Stage-2b): ingested and dived in one pass; settled setup and sizes of the capacity-loss evidence (1.1B, 100B tokens, -0.39 avg / +0.0106 val loss, single run), the fix (scaled KV residual to bottom layer, lambda=35), and that prefill halving is a layer-count identity with no long-context quality evidence. 2026-10-20 second reader (independent agent): re-find 15/15; support yes 5 / partial 0 / no 0; corrections applied: none required.

### Verdict

Cite for: (1) independent small-scale evidence that plain YOCO is slightly worse than a standard Transformer (-0.39 avg points, +0.0106 val loss at 1.1B/100B tokens, single run); (2) a training-time KV-residual fix; (3) early exit halves prefill compute by layer count. Do NOT cite for long-context behaviour, retrofit onto a trained model, MoE, or any claim that YOCO-style skipping is free at inference on a model not trained for it.

### Linked handoffs and actions

- updated `deepseek-v41-flash-evaluation.md`
- updated `kv-unified-stack-rollout.md`
- Recommended action (dive proposal, not an instruction): Operator-review candidate: no code action; use as the independent bound on how much a skipped-upper-layer design can lose at small scale, and as a caution that no long-context quality evidence exists for YOCO-style early exit.

## intake-2129#record — HISA: Efficient Hierarchical Indexing for Fine-Grained Sparse Attention

- **URL**: https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.28458
- **Verification**: dive-verified
- **Verdict**: worth_investigating (novelty medium, relevance medium; categories: inference_serving, kv_cache, hardware_optimization, context_extension)
- **Disposition**: monitor
- **Expanded from**: intake-2117#record

### Claims (as verified)

0. HISA replaces the DSA indexer's flat prefix scan with two stages. Stage 1 scores mean-pooled per-block indexing keys (block size B) using the same indexing queries and head-gating weights as DSA, keeps the top-m blocks plus always the first and last block; stage 2 runs the unchanged DSA token scorer only over the tokens of the kept blocks and takes the final top-k. Output is the same token-index set Sparse MLA already consumes; training-free; token-level KV layout unchanged, with a separate block-pooled indexing-key cache (one pooled vector per block).
   - *unaffected*: algorithm confirmed against §3-§4 and Algorithm 1 pseudocode (App. A)
   - anchor `§1 ¶(Stage 1 bullet)` @ arXiv v4 (10 Sep 2026); html sha256-12 364c3c40c23c: "A pooled representative vector is computed for each block via mean pooling over its constituent indexing keys."
1. Cost model (paper): per query O(L/B + m*B) instead of O(L), per layer O(L^2/B + L*m*B) instead of O(L^2); three regimes (k >= prefix: dense; m*B >= prefix: identical to DSA; m*B << prefix: real pruning). Requires the pooled keys to be maintained incrementally alongside the KV cache.
   - *unaffected*: Eq. 10-11 confirmed
   - anchor `§1 ¶(after Fig.1 desc.)` @ arXiv v4 (10 Sep 2026); html sha256-12 364c3c40c23c: "HISA maintains pooled indexing-key summaries only for its coarse filter."
2. Kernel level (A100, TileLang for BOTH indexers, 64K context, final top-k 2048, B=128): about 2.16x indexer speedup at a 4:1 first-stage compression ratio (16K candidate budget) and up to 3.75x at a fixed 8K candidate budget (m=64). At 64K the DSA indexer costs 5.6 ms against ~1.6 ms for Sparse MLA itself. Kernel microbenchmark only.
   - *unaffected*: 2.16x/3.75x are both stated; the 3.75x is the fixed-8K-budget configuration, not the 4:1 one
   - anchor `§5.1 ¶3` @ arXiv v4 (10 Sep 2026); html sha256-12 364c3c40c23c: "the indexer reaches 5.6 ms at 64K context length"
3. End-to-end (DeepSeek-V3.2, SGLang, 8xH200 TP=8, 10 random requests of 128K input / 512 output, B=128, m=64): throughput 5868.43 -> 9712.21 tok/s (1.65x), TTFT 106449.81 -> 62722.91 ms (1.70x), TPOT 73.02 -> 46.96 ms (1.55x) against the official DSA CUDA path. Gain is concurrency dependent: TPOT speedup is only 1.05x at concurrency 1 (1.38x at 2, 1.55x at 4), TTFT gain flat at about 1.75x. Authors say the HISA kernels are TileLang/Triton and less tuned than the baseline.
   - *unaffected*: Table 1 / Table 4 confirmed; headline 1.65x hides 1.05x TPOT at concurrency 1
   - anchor `App. B.1` @ arXiv v4 (10 Sep 2026); html sha256-12 364c3c40c23c: "The workload consists of 10 random requests, each with 128K input tokens and 512 generated tokens"
4. Quality (training-free drop-in, vLLM FP8, final 2048 tokens for all arms): LongBench average DeepSeek-V3.2 DSA 51.05 / HISA 50.78 / Block-Sparse 49.54; GLM-5 DSA 46.01 / HISA 46.32 / Block-Sparse 42.67 (Block-Sparse = Stage 1 only, 16 blocks of 128). NIAH heatmaps (8K-128K) are qualitative: HISA near DSA with marginal degradation at extreme lengths.
   - *unaffected*: Table 2 confirmed; HISA is -0.27 avg on V3.2 and +0.31 on GLM-5
   - anchor `§5.4 ¶2` @ arXiv v4 (10 Sep 2026); html sha256-12 364c3c40c23c: "all three configurations ultimately retain 2048 tokens for computation"
5. HISA is an approximation, not an exact reproduction: token-set IoU with DSA on DeepSeek-V3.2 is 92.58-94.94% averaged over layer bins but falls with query position from 96.91% (2-4K) to 78.66% (18.4-18.8K); the paper states it approximates rather than exactly reproduces DSA's selection. IoU was only measured to ~18.8K positions, not at the 64K-128K lengths where the speed claims are made.
   - *unaffected*: IoU decay is in the paper; the abstract does not mention it
   - anchor `§5.6 ¶(after Table 3)` @ arXiv v4 (10 Sep 2026); html sha256-12 364c3c40c23c: "HISA therefore preserves the same token-index output contract for Sparse MLA while approximating, rather than exactly reproducing, DSA’s selected token set."
6. Sensitivity: with training-free mean pooling the best block size is non-monotonic and task dependent (candidate pool fixed m*B=8192; B=64/128 edge out 256); authors recommend B=128, m=64, attribute the non-monotonicity to the untrained pooling summary, and list max-pooling / overlapping blocks / training the block scorer as untested future work.
   - *unaffected*: App. C.3 confirmed
   - anchor `App. C.3` @ arXiv v4 (10 Sep 2026); html sha256-12 364c3c40c23c: "there is no single HISA configuration that is optimal everywhere"
7. Relation to DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash (derived by reading the official reference, NOT stated in the paper, which predates V4.1): V4.1's level-1 block score is the MAX over a block of the source layer's own exact index logits (select_candidate_blocks), so building the candidate set costs a full dense index scan at the candidate-source layer (config: layer 20); the saving is in the LATER index-source layers (24, 28, 32, 36), which rescore only inside the 2048x8 = 16384 candidate positions. HISA instead pools KEYS first and scans only N/B pooled rows at the coarse stage, i.e. it would also cut the source-layer scan, at the price of a different (mean-pool) block score the V4.1 weights were not trained against. V4.1's README calls its variant a 'Hierarchical Sparse Indexer' bounded 'independently of context length'.
   - *unaffected*: derived from model.py:L583-L610 and config.json; the paper makes no V4.1 claim
   - anchor `inference/model.py:L597 @ model.py sha256-12 4e9ae23620ed (official V4.1-Flash inference dir; not a git repo)` @ model.py sha256-12 4e9ae23620ed (official V4.1-Flash inference dir; not a git repo): "score each block by its best position; -inf pads the last one out to block_size"
   - anchor `README.md:L49` @ README.md sha256 not pinned; read from disk 2026-10-20: "a **Hierarchical Sparse Indexer** further restricts later indexing layers to a candidate pool constructed by the first Full Mode layer, bounding deeper indexer cost independently of context length"

### Dive corrections

- 2026-10-20 (wave 2, Stage-2b): ingested and dived in one pass; settled that HISA mean-pools index keys while V4.1 maxes exact logits, that HISA is explicitly approximate (IoU decay), and that the 3.75x headline is the fixed-8K-budget kernel config only. 2026-10-20 second reader (independent agent): re-find 9/9; support yes 8 / partial 0 / no 0; corrections applied: none required.

### Verdict

Cite HISA for the block-then-token indexing structure, the O(L/B + mB) cost model and the GPU-measured indexer/e2e speedups (with their conditions: A100 kernel, 8xH200 SGLang, 128K, concurrency-dependent TPOT). Do NOT cite it as exact (IoU 78.7-96.9%), as measured at 128K quality, or as the V4.1 mechanism: V4.1 scores blocks by max of exact logits. The speed numbers are GPU numbers; on EPYC the indexer is a small share of decode bytes next to the dense-mask final attention (see ledger HISA-A1).

### Linked handoffs and actions

- updated `deepseek-v41-flash-evaluation.md`
- Recommended action (dive proposal, not an instruction): Operator-review candidate: do NOT port HISA as such; port the exact V4.1 candidate restriction (indexer rescoring only inside the candidate blocks on index-source layers 24/28/32/36) and treat HISA pooled-key scanning for the source layer / ratio-2 layers as an optional approximate variant gated by ppl and top-k IoU against the reference.

## intake-2130#record — You Only Index Once: Cross-Layer Sparse Attention with Shared Routing

- **URL**: https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06467
- **Verification**: dive-verified
- **Verdict**: worth_investigating (novelty medium, relevance low; categories: inference_serving, kv_cache, context_extension)
- **Disposition**: integrated
- **Expanded from**: intake-2117#record

### Claims (as verified)

0. What is shared: one single-head indexer (index q/k projected from the shared hidden states at the YOCO self-decoder/cross-decoder boundary) computes a token-level top-k (activated tokens up to 2048) ONCE per token, and all 16 cross-decoder layers reuse that same index over the same shared KV cache. Each layer still has its own queries and FFN. The paper's own name for the method is cross-layer sparse attention (CLSA).
   - *unaffected*: Fig.1 / §2 / §2.1 confirmed; the abstract says "up to 2048" via §3.1
   - anchor `§1 ¶4` @ arXiv v1 (04 Jun 2026); html sha256-12 dde9e10201cd: "When multiple cross-decoder layers read from the same KV cache, they also share the same routing index."
1. It is an architecture plus training result, not a retrofit: a 4B model (32 layers = 16 self-decoder + 16 cross-decoder, hidden 2560, 20 heads / 4 KV heads, SWA 512 in the self-decoder) is pretrained dense (125,000 + 10,000 steps at 8M tokens/step) and then sparse-adapted for 2,500 + 2,500 steps at 32K using a multi-layer KL distillation of the indexer onto the head-and-layer-averaged dense cross-attention distribution (lambda 0.1). The sharing only exists because all cross-decoder layers read ONE KV cache.
   - *unaffected*: training schedule from Table 5 / §3.1
   - anchor `§3.1 ¶1` @ arXiv v1 (04 Jun 2026); html sha256-12 dde9e10201cd: "We compare a Transformer baseline, a dense YOCO model, and YOCO with cross-sparse attention, all at the 4B scale."
2. Quality (4B, vs dense YOCO and vs a Transformer): Table 2 average over 8 tasks roughly equal to dense YOCO (e.g. GSM8K 0.470 vs 0.430, MMLU 0.513 vs 0.519); RULER average 62.9 vs 62.7 (16K) and 53.1 vs 52.3 (32K), Transformer 64.4 / 46.2; long-context validation loss overlaps dense YOCO. At 2048 selected tokens the routed set covers 76.29-84.12% of dense attention mass and the CE-loss gap to dense is -0.0004 (StarCoder) to +0.0054 (Books).
   - *unaffected*: Tables 2-4 confirmed
   - anchor `§3.5 ¶2` @ arXiv v1 (04 Jun 2026); html sha256-12 dde9e10201cd: "Across Books, ArXiv, and StarCoder, sparse attention reaches dense-level quality, with loss differences around"
3. The quality COST OF SHARING is not isolated: there is no ablation that holds the architecture fixed and compares one shared index against per-layer (DSA-style) indices, and no cross-layer index-similarity measurement. The introduction's statement that attention-pattern analysis shows sharing has only a minor effect rests on the coverage table (Table 4) alone.
   - *unaffected*: Stage-1 claim that sharing is shown to be cheap is NOT supported by an isolating experiment
   - anchor `§1 ¶5` @ arXiv v1 (04 Jun 2026); html sha256-12 dde9e10201cd: "Attention-pattern analysis further shows that sharing one routing index across layers has only a minor effect on the resulting attention behavior"
4. Speed (vLLM with the authors' CLSA merged in, NVIDIA B200, 4B model): at 128K decode throughput 3276.80 vs 431.16 tok/s for the Transformer (7.6x) and vs 960.94 for dense YOCO (3.4x); overall 1068.06 vs 62.53 tok/s (17.1x; vs dense YOCO 1.8x). Per-layer latency at 128K: Transformer 2.28 ms, YOCO dense 1.04, YOCO CLSA 0.31, of which amortized top-k 0.08 ms and sparse attention 0.05. The baseline for the headline ratios is the dense Transformer, not DSA/IndexCache.
   - *unaffected*: 7.6x = 3276.80/431.16 and 17.1x = 1068.06/62.53 recomputed from Tables 11-12; baseline is a dense Transformer
   - anchor `§3.4 ¶3` @ arXiv v1 (04 Jun 2026); html sha256-12 dde9e10201cd: "All numbers in this subsection are obtained on NVIDIA B200 GPUs."
5. Cost model (Table 1): decode O(L/2 (W1+W2) D + eta*N) for CLSA versus O(L W2 D + eta*L*N) for per-layer DSA, i.e. the indexer term is paid once instead of once per layer. The paper's contrast set: DSA (per-layer top-k; slower than dense Transformer at 128K), IndexCache (reuse across four layers), HySparse (block-sparse layers mixed with dense layers) - all lose to CLSA on per-layer latency at 128K (Fig. 5, no numbers in the text for them).
   - *unaffected*: Table 1, Fig. 5 narrative
   - anchor `§3.4 ¶(Fig. 5)` @ arXiv v1 (04 Jun 2026); html sha256-12 dde9e10201cd: "At 128K context, DSA is slower than the dense Transformer because the unamortized token top- $k$ dominates per-layer cost."
6. Contrast with DeepSeek-V4.1 CSA2 (derived from model.py/config.json, not from the paper): V4.1 shares at finer grain with three static layer modes - Full (index source that also owns KV, layers 2/8/14/20), Reindex (index source rescoring inside the candidate pool, layers 24/28/32/36) and Reuse (copies the source's topk_idxs, the other layers) - using a multi-head indexer (32 heads x 128 dim, fp4) with its own weights per source, so there are 8 index scans per token, not 1. YOIO has one single-head indexer for 16 layers with mean-attention distillation. V4.1 keeps the layer-specific query for attention but the sparse index comes from a recent source layer.
   - *unaffected*: derived from model.py:L488-L581, L720-L735 and config.json
   - anchor `inference/model.py:L724-L727 @ model.py sha256-12 4e9ae23620ed (official V4.1-Flash inference dir; not a git repo)` @ model.py sha256-12 4e9ae23620ed (official V4.1-Flash inference dir; not a git repo): "the layers in between reuse the result their source published."
   - anchor `README.md:L49` @ README.md read from disk 2026-10-20: "assigns each attention layer one of three static modes — **Full**, **Reindex**, or **Reuse** — to share main KV and indexer K across layers and reuse Top-K sparse-attention indices"

### Dive corrections

- 2026-10-20 (wave 2, Stage-2b): ingested and dived in one pass; settled that the shared routing is one single-head index over a YOCO shared KV cache, that its sharing cost is not isolated in the paper, and that the headline speedups are versus a dense Transformer. 2026-10-20 second reader (independent agent): re-find 8/8; support yes 7 / partial 0 / no 0; corrections applied: none required.

### Verdict

Cite YOIO/CLSA for: one shared single-head indexer feeding 16 YOCO cross-decoder layers, trained by distillation; and for the GPU observation that per-layer token top-k can exceed dense attention time at 128K. Do NOT cite it for the quality cost of sharing (not isolated), for retrofitting existing models (needs YOCO + training), or for the 7.6x/17.1x as gains over sparse baselines (they are over a dense Transformer on B200).

### Linked handoffs and actions

- updated `deepseek-v41-flash-evaluation.md`
- Recommended action (dive proposal, not an instruction): Operator-review candidate: knowledge-only for EPYC. The method needs a YOCO-architecture model trained for it, so it does not apply to DS41 or the Qwen lineup; its use is as independent evidence that cross-layer index sharing is cheap in quality terms only when the model is trained for it (V4.1 is), and that a per-layer top-k dominates decode cost on GPU.

## intake-2131#record — CONCUR: High-Throughput Agentic Batch Inference of LLM via Congestion-Based Concurrency Control

- **URL**: https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.22705
- **Verification**: dive-verified
- **Verdict**: worth_investigating (novelty medium, relevance medium; categories: inference_serving, kv_cache, agent_architecture)
- **Disposition**: integrated
- **Expanded from**: intake-2119#record

### Claims (as verified)

0. PATHOLOGY (vendor-reported, authors' H100 + SGLang traces): agentic batch inference shows three phases - warmup (hit rate near 90%), a 'middle phase' that is >90% of runtime where KV usage sits at ~80-100% while the prefix-cache hit rate collapses (LRU evicts the KV of agents paused on tool calls, so resumed agents re-prefill their whole history; recomputation = 49.1% of end-to-end latency in the DeepSeek-V3 trace), and a cooldown. Adding agents in the middle phase LOWERS throughput.
   - *unaffected*: phenomenon confirmed from §3.2/Fig 3
   - anchor `§3.2 ¶3 (Middle Phase)` @ arXiv 2601.22705v1 (30 Jan 2026): "this extra recomputation takes 49.1% of the end-to-end latency"
   - anchor `§3.2 ¶3` @ arXiv 2601.22705v1 (30 Jan 2026): "the prolonged middle-phase thrashing that dominates over 90% of the total execution time"
   - anchor `§3.2 ¶2 (Warmup)` @ arXiv 2601.22705v1 (30 Jan 2026): "KV cache hit rates approach 90%"
1. CONTROL LAW: AIMD on the number of concurrently admitted AGENTS (window W), not requests. W += alpha when KV usage U < U_low; W *= beta when U > U_high AND hit rate H < H_thresh; otherwise hold. Signals: KV cache usage (proactive) and cache hit rate (reactive; the analogue of packet loss = eviction/recompute). Constants fixed for all models/engines: alpha=2, beta=0.5, U_low=0.2, U_high=0.5, H_thresh=0.2. U_high is robust over 0.5-0.6 (0.8 gives 4-5x latency, 0.4 gives +46-64%); U_low is the sensitive one (0.1 gives 7-8x latency, 0.3/0.5 give 2-3x).
   - *unaffected*: law and constants confirmed §4.3/§5.1/§A.1
   - anchor `§4.3 Algorithm Design` @ arXiv 2601.22705v1 (30 Jan 2026): "the KV Cache Usage"
   - anchor `§5.1 Hyperparameter Configuration` @ arXiv 2601.22705v1 (30 Jan 2026): "we set the additive increase factor to"
   - anchor `§A.1 Impact of U_low` @ arXiv 2601.22705v1 (30 Jan 2026): "requires more careful calibration to ensure the controller neither under-utilizes capacity nor over-admits requests"
2. MECHANISM: a middleware controller between agent framework and serving engine with three primitives admit/pause/resume; pause is applied only at generation-step/tool-call boundaries and preserves agent state; paused agents' idle KV is protected simply by not admitting new work while pressure is high. No engine changes (SGLang used).
   - *unaffected*: primitives confirmed §4.3
   - anchor `§4.3 Agent-Level Control Primitives` @ arXiv 2601.22705v1 (30 Jan 2026): "Pause temporarily suspends an agent from issuing further requests while preserving its execution state"
3. RESULTS (vendor-reported, one paper, H100 80GB NVLink, SGLang, fixed alpha/beta/thresholds): offline batch latency vs plain SGLang up to 4.09x on Qwen3-32B (batch 256, TP8/8 GPUs: 1480 s -> 362 s) and up to 1.90x on DeepSeek-V3 (batch 40, TP16: 3877 s -> 2043 s); at low pressure the gain is small (DeepSeek-V3 batch 32: 1.20x). The fixed request-level cap baseline was WORSE than plain SGLang in two configs (0.72x Qwen3-32B TP8; 0.90x DS-V3 batch 32), and HiCache CPU offload was worse than baseline at DS-V3 batch 16/32 (0.34x/0.54x). Best fixed agent cap (30/32/64/128) was 1.5-2.9x slower than the adaptive controller on Qwen3-32B batch 256 TP2.
   - *unaffected*: Table 1 numbers confirmed; ratios are latency, config-dependent
   - anchor `§5.1` @ arXiv 2601.22705v1 (30 Jan 2026): "Concur reduces latency by up to 4.09 × 4.09\times compared to SGLang and 2.8 × 2.8\times compared to request-level admission."
   - anchor `§5.1 setup` @ arXiv 2601.22705v1 (30 Jan 2026): "All experiments are conducted on NVIDIA H100 GPUs (80GB)"
4. HIT-RATE FIGURES (DeepSeek-V3, TP8): plain SGLang 80.38/77.72/35.41 % at batch 16/32/40; request-cap 69.00/68.39/32.21 %; HiCache 97.48/97.13/96.08 %; CONCUR 96.38/93.65/73.36 %. These are the engine's prefix-cache hit rate UNDER the compared policies, not a property of the agentic trace itself. NOTE: the paper contains no '>=95% agentic KV hit rate' statement; DualPath's citation of it for that figure (intake-2119#record) is not supported by this source.
   - *unaffected*: Table 2 confirmed; no >=95% agentic figure
   - anchor `§5.2 ¶2` @ arXiv 2601.22705v1 (30 Jan 2026): "they collapse to 35.41 % 35.41\% at the configuration of a large batch size of 40"
5. SCOPE LIMITS: offline batch (RL rollout / distillation / evaluation) with a fixed agent population, GPU-resident KV, LRU prefix tree; no SLO/latency-percentile evaluation, no interactive or mixed traffic, no CPU inference, single paper, no code release found in the text, thresholds hand-set (sensitivity shown only for Qwen3-32B TP8/4/2). Arxiv abs lists the title 'CONCUR: High-Throughput Agentic Batch Inference of LLM via Congestion-Based Concurrency Control' while the v1 HTML body is titled 'Concur: Proactive Agent-Level Admission Control for Efficient Agentic Batch Inference' (same arXiv id, v1 only).
   - *unaffected*: limits are absences, verified by full read

### Dive corrections

- 2026-10-20 (wave 2, Stage-2b): ingested and dived in one pass; settled the signal (KV usage + hit rate, two-signal AIMD on agent count), the constants, the Table 1/2 numbers with conditions, and that DualPath ">95% hit rate" is NOT in CONCUR; surfaced that llama-server lacks a usage gauge so the signal must be derived from /slots. 2026-10-20 second reader (independent agent): re-find 10/10; support yes 5 / partial 0 / no 0; corrections applied: none required.

### Verdict

Cite CONCUR for ONE thing: an AIMD agent-admission window gated on (KV usage high AND hit rate low) beat fixed caps on GPU+SGLang offline batch, with numbers that are vendor-reported and configuration dependent. Do NOT cite it for an agentic hit-rate figure (it has none; DualPath own 98.7% is separate) and do NOT carry its constants or its 4.09x/1.90x to llama-server. Our request-level pool admission already exists; the open question is whether AGENT-level resume recompute is measurable on :8083 at all (np4 plus --cache-ram changes the eviction economics).

### Linked handoffs and actions

- updated `kv-unified-stack-rollout.md`
- Recommended action (dive proposal, not an instruction): Probe first: measure resume-turn recompute fraction (n_prompt_tokens_processed / n_prompt_tokens from /slots + timings) on :8083 np4 under real 4-agent fan-out before building any controller (ledger CONCUR-A1).
- Recommended action (dive proposal, not an instruction): Do not adopt the paper constants; if a controller is built, calibrate U_low/U_high on our pool and add a hit-rate gate to the existing SharedKVPoolAdmission.

## intake-2132#record — XGrammar: Flexible and Efficient Structured Generation Engine for Large Language Models

- **URL**: https://arxiv.org/abs/2411.15100
- **Verification**: dive-verified
- **Verdict**: worth_investigating (novelty medium, relevance medium; categories: inference_serving, speculative_decoding, tool_implementation)
- **Disposition**: integrated
- **Expanded from**: intake-2116#record

### Claims (as verified)

0. TOKEN-MASK PRECOMPUTE: a byte-level pushdown automaton for the CFG; per automaton node, vocabulary tokens are split into context-independent (validity decided from the stack-top node alone: matching only expands into a child rule or advances within the rule) and context-dependent (matching pops past the end of the rule so the full stack is needed). Context-independent accept/reject sets are precomputed into an 'adaptive token mask cache' keyed by stack-top node; only the context-dependent remainder is checked at runtime. Llama-3.1 + JSON grammar: context-dependent tokens <1% (1134 of 128k); context expansion (per-rule 'expanded suffix' automata) cuts them 90% to 120; adaptive storage (store the smaller of accepted/rejected set, bitset only when balanced) cuts cache memory 160 MB -> 0.46 MB (0.2%).
   - *unaffected*: mechanism confirmed in paper and code comments
   - anchor `§3.1 ¶1` @ arXiv 2411.15100v3 (12 May 2025, MLSys 2025): "Validating tokens in the former two cases only relies on the stack top node"
   - anchor `§3.1 ¶2` @ arXiv 2411.15100v3 (12 May 2025, MLSys 2025): "amounting to less than 1% (1134 out of 128k) for the Llama-3.1 model using JSON grammar"
   - anchor `§3.2 ¶3` @ arXiv 2411.15100v3 (12 May 2025, MLSys 2025): "this technique reduces context-dependent tokens by 90% (from 1,134 to 120)"
   - anchor `§3.1 Adaptive storage` @ arXiv 2411.15100v3 (12 May 2025, MLSys 2025): "this adaptive storage method can effectively reduce the total memory usage to 0.2% (from 160 MB to 0.46 MB)"
1. MASK COST PER STEP (vendor-reported, authors' benchmarks; Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct, Ryzen 9 7950X + RTX 4090): under 40 us per token for JSON Schema and CFG(JSON), under 200 us for XML and Python-DSL CFG; up to 3x faster than the best baseline on JSON Schema and >100x on CFG; baselines were Outlines v1.0, llama.cpp grammar engine b3998 and lm-format-enforcer. Ablation (CFG JSON, Llama-3.1-8B): PDA baseline 65.8 ms/token -> node merging 38.3 -> adaptive token mask cache 0.154 -> rule inlining 0.035 -> context expansion 0.018 ms/token (the cache is the dominant step, 248.6x).
   - *narrowed*: numbers hold only for the authors hardware and Llama-3.1 128k tokenizer
   - anchor `§4.1 ¶2` @ arXiv 2411.15100v3 (12 May 2025, MLSys 2025): "with under 40 µs per token for JSON Schema and CFG (JSON), and under 200 µs for XML and Python DSL"
   - anchor `§4.1 ¶1` @ arXiv 2411.15100v3 (12 May 2025, MLSys 2025): "the grammar engine in llama.cpp Gerganov (2023) (b3998)"
   - anchor `§4.3 Table 3 text` @ arXiv 2411.15100v3 (12 May 2025, MLSys 2025): "the results show that the adaptive token mask cache has the greatest impact in terms of speedup"
2. WHY llama.cpp IS SLOW IN THIS COMPARISON: the paper characterises llama.cpp's grammar engine as a PDA that checks the ENTIRE vocabulary at runtime every step (no precompute). End-to-end (Llama-3.1-8B, H100, batch sweep) the paper reports vLLM+Outlines and llama.cpp throughput limited by grammar CPU time, with XGrammar giving up to 80x output-token-rate; MLC-LLM TPOT with vs without XGrammar is 6.2 vs 6.3 ms (batch 1) and 9.0 vs 9.2 ms (batch 16) on JSON Schema - near-zero overhead because mask generation (CPU) is overlapped with the GPU forward pass.
   - *unaffected*: paper statement about llama.cpp b3998 confirmed; applicability to our tree is NOT established
   - anchor `§5 Related Work` @ arXiv 2411.15100v3 (12 May 2025, MLSys 2025): "but requires checking the entire vocabulary at runtime, which incurs large overhead"
   - anchor `§3.5` @ arXiv 2411.15100v3 (12 May 2025, MLSys 2025): "we overlap the computation for mask generation with the LLM inference process"
3. ROLLBACK / SPECULATIVE API: a persistent execution stack (tree of stacks) gives O(1) state branching and rollback by moving stack-top pointers; the paper says a sliding window of history is kept and names speculative decoding trees (SpecInfer) and jump-forward retokenization as consumers. In the code at HEAD, GrammarMatcher.rollback(num_tokens) exists and the constructor argument max_rollback_tokens is DEPRECATED: any value other than -1 raises a DeprecationWarning and the effective limit is always unlimited (-1). DeepInfer still passes max_rollback_tokens = DEEPINFER_USE_NEXTN_DECODE + 2 and also keeps its own capped trigger-progress history of the same length, so the cap is a DeepInfer-side invariant, not an xgrammar limit at this revision.
   - *narrowed*: rollback exists but max_rollback_tokens is deprecated/unlimited at HEAD; DeepInfer cap is local
   - anchor `§3.3 ¶3` @ arXiv 2411.15100v3 (12 May 2025, MLSys 2025): "At runtime, a sliding window of history is maintained. To roll back to a previous state, we only need to change the current stack pointers, which requires constant time."
   - anchor `§3.3 ¶5` @ arXiv 2411.15100v3 (12 May 2025, MLSys 2025): "We can maintain the automata state for every branch of the output tree"
   - anchor `python/xgrammar/matcher.py:L261-L266 @ 614ab01` @ 614ab01: ""max_rollback_tokens is deprecated. You don't need to set it and it's always ""
   - anchor `deepinfer/structure_output/matcher.py:L20 @ 774ce24` @ 774ce24: "MAX_ROLLBACK_TOKENS = envs.DEEPINFER_USE_NEXTN_DECODE + 2"
   - anchor `deepinfer/structure_output/matcher.py:L83 @ 774ce24` @ 774ce24: "self.inner_matcher = InnerMatcher(ctx, max_rollback_tokens=MAX_ROLLBACK_TOKENS)"
4. QUALITY EFFECT (vendor-reported, Llama-3.1-8B, authors' datasets): syntactic correctness with vs without grammar: function calling 62% -> 100%, XML 80% -> 100% (json-mode-eval and a synthetic XML set). Says nothing about task accuracy.
   - *unaffected*: Table 4 confirmed; syntactic only
   - anchor `§4.4 Table 4 text` @ arXiv 2411.15100v3 (12 May 2025, MLSys 2025): "XGrammar ensures 100% syntactic correctness of the generated outputs."
5. PROVENANCE AND SUPERSESSION: canonical source github.com/mlc-ai/xgrammar (Apache-style project; HEAD 614ab01 dated 2026-10-17) and the MLSys 2025 paper arXiv 2411.15100 'XGrammar: Flexible and Efficient Structured Generation Engine for Large Language Models' (Dong, Ruan, Cai, Lai, Xu, Zhao, Chen; v1 22 Nov 2024, v3 12 May 2025). The repo README additionally cites a 2026 follow-up 'XGrammar-2: Dynamic and Efficient Structured Generation Engine for Agentic LLMs' (arXiv 2601.04426, observed in the arXiv API); the paper measures a 2024 engine, the repo at HEAD has since grown Lark grammars, captures and a BatchGrammarMatcher (matcher.py:L561-L711), none of which the 2024 paper's numbers cover.
   - *unaffected*: provenance confirmed on arxiv.org/abs and README

### Dive corrections

- 2026-10-20 (wave 2, Stage-2b): ingested and dived in one pass; resolved canonical source (mlc-ai/xgrammar + arXiv 2411.15100 title verified on abs page), settled mask precompute and per-step cost, and OVERTURNED the reading of max_rollback_tokens as an engine bound (deprecated, unlimited at HEAD 614ab01); mapped llama.cpp grammar sampling and spec verify at dd3d9721c. 2026-10-20 second reader (independent agent): re-find 15/15; support yes 5 / partial 0 / no 0; corrections applied: none required.

### Verdict

Cite XGrammar for the mechanism (context-independent token masks precomputed per automaton position; only the <1% context-dependent remainder checked at runtime; O(1) persistent-stack rollback) and for its authors own tens-of-microseconds mask costs on a 128k vocabulary. Do NOT cite max_rollback_tokens as an engine limit (deprecated, unlimited at HEAD), do NOT read the >100x llama.cpp gap as applying to our newer tree, and do NOT assume a mask-overlap benefit on CPU-inference models. llama.cpp verify already advances the grammar only on accepted tokens, so spec decoding + grammar is a cost question (one grammar sample per verify row), not a correctness gap.

### Linked handoffs and actions

- updated `speculative-decoding-mtp-refresh.md`
- Recommended action (dive proposal, not an instruction): Do not build anything yet: first measure what llama.cpp grammar sampling actually costs on our vocabularies and whether any production path uses a grammar with MTP/DFlash verify (ledger XGRAMMAR-A1).
- Recommended action (dive proposal, not an instruction): Treat XGrammar-2 (arXiv 2601.04426) as the better-matched follow-up for agentic tool-call workloads (surfaced).

## intake-2133#record — DeepGEMM-Ascend: Clean and Efficient BLAS Kernel Library on Ascend NPU

- **URL**: https://github.com/deepseek-ai/DeepGEMM-Ascend
- **Verification**: dive-verified
- **Verdict**: adopt_patterns (novelty medium, relevance medium; categories: hardware_optimization, quantization, moe_optimization)
- **Disposition**: monitor
- **Expanded from**: intake-2123#record

### Claims (as verified)

0. Full Ascend 950 (CANN 9.20) port of DeepGEMM, API-compatible: BF16/FP8/FP4 dense GEMM, FP8xFP4 mixed GEMM, M-grouped and K-grouped GEMM, MQA logits (dense + paged, for the Lightning Indexer), MegaMoE, and an HC-Prenorm GEMM for the mHC (hyper-connections) module.
   - *unaffected*: New entry (Stage-2b); no prior Stage-1 claim to correct.
   - anchor `README.md:L3 @ 3a35326` @ 3a35326: "DeepGEMM Ascend is a port of DeepGEMM to the HUAWEI Ascend platform. It is fully API-compatible with DeepGEMM and supports BF16, FP8, FP4 GEMM, MQA logits, and MegaMoE."
1. Mixed-precision FP8xFP4 GEMM is a DISTINCT kernel (fp8_dequant_gemm) from the native pure-FP4xFP4 GEMM: FP4 (E2M1, 2 values/byte) operands are unpacked to FP8 (E4M3) via a 256-entry register lookup table built once per kernel (vf_build_lut) and a vectorized gather+nibble-interleave (vf_deq_nd2nz) before entering the systolic MAD array; the LUT directly maps each 4-bit E2M1 code to its E4M3 bit pattern (code 0 -> 0x00, code 1 -> 0x30, codes 2-7 -> 0x30 + code<<2, sign bit relocated), i.e. dequant is a table lookup, not float arithmetic.
   - *unaffected*: New entry; clarified the dequant mechanism is LUT-based, and that pure FP4xFP4 also runs natively (dequant path is specific to the mixed-dtype case).
   - anchor `csrc/apis/gemm.hpp:L28-L29 @ 3a35326` @ 3a35326: "} else if (ab_dtype == std::tie(torch::kFloat8_e4m3fn, kPackedFP4)) { deep_gemm::launch_fp8_dequant_gemm(desc);"
   - anchor `deep_gemm/include/deep_gemm/fp8_dequant_gemm.hpp:L13-L14 @ 3a35326` @ 3a35326: "Each packed FP4 byte holds two E2M1 values. Dequantization splits the low and high nibbles and uses each as an index into this LUT, producing two raw E4M3 bytes."
   - anchor `deep_gemm/include/deep_gemm/fp8_dequant_gemm.hpp:L24-L25 @ 3a35326` @ 3a35326: "E2M1 code 0 maps to E4M3 0x00, code 1 to 0x30, and codes 2..7 to 0x30 + (code << 2). FP4 sign bit 3 is moved to E4M3 sign bit 7 below."
   - anchor `deep_gemm/include/deep_gemm/fp8_dequant_gemm.hpp:L69 @ 3a35326` @ 3a35326: "A 256B load holds 512 FP4 values. Low nibbles are even columns; high nibbles are odd columns."
2. Pure FP4xFP4 dense GEMM IS run natively on Ascend 950 hardware (no dequant stage) and reaches 98.3% of the reported FP4 hardware TFLOPS ceiling, so the dequant-to-FP8 path exists only for the mixed FP8xFP4 operand case, not because the hardware lacks FP4 MAD entirely.
   - *unaffected*: New entry.
   - anchor `README.md:L110 @ 3a35326` @ 3a35326: "| FP4×FP4 | 4096 | 7168 | 16384 | 565.6 | 1701 | 1730 | 98.3 |"
3. Scaling-factor (UE8M0, power-of-two) packing differs from NVIDIA DeepGEMM: pairs along K are packed into one int16 (vs 4-per-int32 on SM100) and stored MN-major for Ascend DMA/cache efficiency; the exponent encoding itself (same UE8M0 semantics) is unchanged.
   - *unaffected*: New entry.
   - anchor `README.md:L56 @ 3a35326` @ 3a35326: "The scaling factor format on Ascend differs from NVIDIA's: each pair of UE8M0 scaling factors along the K dimension is packed into an `int16`, and the packed values are stored in MN-major order for optimal hardware efficiency."
4. MegaMoE (EP dispatch + grouped L1/L2 GEMM + SwiGLU + combine, fused into one kernel) is ported to Ascend with its own MMA pipeline config (MMAConfig: BLOCK_M/N/K, MAD_M/N/K, L1/L0/epilogue stage counts) and benchmarked at EP8, top-k=6 + 1 shared expert, up to 384 experts / 16384 tokens-per-rank; only absolute latency/TFLOPS/bandwidth are reported, no fused-vs-unfused speedup figure (unlike upstream DeepGEMM PR #316).
   - *unaffected*: New entry; noted vendor-only, unreleased-hardware caveat.
   - anchor `README.md:L162 @ 3a35326` @ 3a35326: "Mega MoE fuses EP dispatch, two grouped GEMMs, SwiGLU, and combine. Benchmarked over EP8 with top-k=6 (each token routed to 6 experts) and one shared expert; all values are averaged across 8 ranks."
5. Grouped GEMM for MoE experts takes the same two-layout split as upstream DeepGEMM (contiguous for prefill, PSUM/masked-equivalent "WithPsumLayout" for decode under fixed shapes), confirmed by the GemmType enum (Normal, Batched, MGroupedContiguousWithPsumLayout, KGroupedContiguousWithPsumLayout) shared across the FP8 and FP8-dequant kernel families.
6. MQA-logits kernel reported as FIX-pipe bound (99% utilization of a non-MAD execution pipe), not compute-bound, at the benchmarked prefill/decode shapes -- a different bottleneck class than the GEMM kernels.

### Dive corrections

- 2026-10-20 (wave 2, Stage-2b): ingested and dived in one pass. Settled the brief's anchors: kernel inventory (BF16/FP8/FP4 dense GEMM, FP8xFP4 mixed GEMM, M/K-grouped GEMM, MQA logits dense+paged, MegaMoE, HC-Prenorm) confirmed from the file tree and README; FP8/FP4 hardware handling resolved as dequant-to-FP8-via-register-LUT for the MIXED FP8xFP4 case specifically (pure FP4xFP4 has its own native kernel and is NOT dequanted); what was rewritten vs shared with DeepGEMM is the GemmType/layout API surface (shared design, Ascend-specific MAD/pipeline/SF-packing internals); performance numbers recorded with hardware and conditions, flagged vendor-only/unreleased-hardware, and MegaMoE here carries no baseline comparison unlike intake-2123#record's PR #316. 2026-10-20 second reader (independent agent): re-find 8/8; support yes 2 / partial 0 / no 3. The three 'no' verdicts (claims 2, 3, 4) were rotated anchors (each claim carried a neighbouring claim's README quote); main verified and re-indexed README:L110 -> claim 2, README:L56 -> claim 3, README:L162 -> claim 4; quotes and hashes unchanged. Main also dropped a malformed bare-list depends_on [intake-2123#record]: the counterfactual test fails (this entry's claims rest on the DeepGEMM-Ascend repo, not on intake-2123#record's claims).

### Verdict

Cite this repo for ONE thing only: the concrete, hardware-proven pattern of dequantizing a packed sub-byte/narrow format (FP4/E2M1) into a wider native-MAD format (FP8/E4M3) via a small REGISTER-RESIDENT LOOKUP TABLE plus a vectorized nibble-gather, run inline before the matmul, rather than a float-arithmetic dequant loop. Do NOT cite its TFLOPS/utilization numbers as portable evidence (Ascend 950 is a different, non-public-benchmark NPU with its own MAD/FIX-pipe microarchitecture) and do NOT cite the MegaMoE figures as a fused-vs-unfused speedup claim (this repo reports no baseline, unlike intake-2123#record's PR #316).

### Linked handoffs and actions

- updated `deepseek-v41-flash-evaluation.md`
- updated `moe-routing-tap-and-locality-measurement.md`

## intake-2134#record — deepseek-ai/EPLB - Expert Parallelism Load Balancer (redundant-expert replication and placement planner)

- **URL**: https://github.com/deepseek-ai/EPLB
- **Verification**: dive-verified
- **Verdict**: worth_investigating (novelty medium, relevance medium; categories: moe_optimization, inference_serving, hardware_optimization)
- **Disposition**: integrated
- **Expanded from**: intake-2116#record

### Claims (as verified)

0. Policy choice is a one-line switch on divisibility, not on load or stage: hierarchical when num_groups % num_nodes == 0, otherwise 'global', which is the SAME hierarchical routine called with one group and one node (eplb.py:L150-L156). The README says hierarchical suits prefill with smaller EP and global suits decode with larger EP, but that is guidance; the code does not look at the stage.
   - *unaffected*: Stage-1 summary of two policies confirmed; added that the switch is divisibility only, and that DS41 has no groups.
   - anchor `eplb.py:L150 @ d52c72d` @ deepseek-ai/EPLB @ d52c72d5b2f2fb4c41afbf8eb21_366_820_239_913d: "if num_groups % num_nodes == 0:"
   - anchor `eplb.py:L156 @ d52c72d` @ deepseek-ai/EPLB @ d52c72d5b2f2fb4c41afbf8eb21_366_820_239_913d: "phy2log, phyrank, logcnt = rebalance_experts_hierarchical(weight, num_replicas, 1, 1, num_gpus)"
   - anchor `README.md §Hierarchical Load Balancing ¶1 @ d52c72d` @ deepseek-ai/EPLB @ d52c72d5b2f2fb4c41afbf8eb21_366_820_239_913d: "The hierarchical load balancing policy can be used in prefilling stage with a smaller expert-parallel size."
1. Replica-count rule: the caller fixes the total physical-expert count; redundant = num_phy - num_log (must be >= 0, and num_replicas must be a multiple of num_gpus); every logical expert starts with 1 copy and the redundant slots are handed out ONE AT A TIME to whichever expert currently has the highest load per replica (argmax of weight/logcnt), per layer, with no cap and no gain threshold (eplb.py:L58-L71). So replica counts follow the load skew greedily; there is no separate 'how many replicas does this expert deserve' formula.
   - *unaffected*: Replica rule read from code.
   - anchor `eplb.py:L67 @ d52c72d` @ deepseek-ai/EPLB @ d52c72d5b2f2fb4c41afbf8eb21_366_820_239_913d: "redundant_indices = (weight / logcnt).max(dim=-1).indices"
   - anchor `eplb.py:L59 @ d52c72d` @ deepseek-ai/EPLB @ d52c72d5b2f2fb4c41afbf8eb21_366_820_239_913d: "num_redundant = num_phy - num_log"
2. Packing algorithm (balanced_packing): longest-processing-time greedy with an equal-cardinality constraint. Sort items by weight descending, put each into the least-loaded pack that is not yet full (each pack gets exactly n/m items); pure Python loop per layer; shortcut when one item per pack (eplb.py:L5-L41). It optimises the mean weight vector only: one number per expert per layer.
   - *unaffected*: Packing algorithm read from code.
   - anchor `eplb.py:L34 @ d52c72d` @ deepseek-ai/EPLB @ d52c72d5b2f2fb4c41afbf8eb21_366_820_239_913d: "pack = min((i for i in range(num_packs) if pack_items[i] < groups_per_pack),"
   - anchor `eplb.py:L27 @ d52c72d` @ deepseek-ai/EPLB @ d52c72d5b2f2fb4c41afbf8eb21_366_820_239_913d: "indices = weight.float().sort(-1, descending=True).indices.cpu()"
3. Hierarchical flow (eplb.py:L74-L129): (1) sum expert load per expert-group and pack GROUPS onto nodes with balanced_packing; (2) inside each node replicate experts with replicate_experts; (3) pack the replicas onto that node's GPUs with balanced_packing using per-replica load = expert load / replica count (assumes the replicas split the load evenly). Global policy skips (1). Group-aware placement exists to cut inter-node traffic under DeepSeek-V3 group-limited routing (README).
   - *unaffected*: Hierarchical flow confirmed.
   - anchor `eplb.py:L117 @ d52c72d` @ deepseek-ai/EPLB @ d52c72d5b2f2fb4c41afbf8eb21_366_820_239_913d: "tokens_per_phy = (tokens_per_mlog / mlogcnt).gather(-1, phy2mlog)"
4. The repo contains only the planner (eplb.py 164 lines, README, MIT licence, one example image). The load predictor is explicitly out of scope; README suggests a moving average of historical statistics. No token-to-replica assignment/dispatch rule, no weight-movement code, no tests, and no benchmark or balance-quality numbers are in the repo. The README worked example (two layers, 12 experts, 16 replicas, 2 nodes x 4 GPUs) prints a placement but no balance metric.
   - *narrowed*: Source is a planner only: no dispatch rule, no movement code, no metrics, so it cannot be cited for any balance-quality or speedup number.
   - anchor `README.md §intro ¶2 @ d52c72d` @ deepseek-ai/EPLB @ d52c72d5b2f2fb4c41afbf8eb21_366_820_239_913d: "Note that the exact method to predict the loads of experts is out of this repo's scope."
5. Differences from DeepInfer's eplb/policy.py (read in the seed dive, D4, @774ce24): (a) input is a single aggregate load vector [layers, experts] here versus a WINDOW [steps, layers, experts] there; (b) replica choice here = hottest per-replica expert by aggregate, there = the expert whose extra replica minimises the SUM OVER STEPS of the worst per-replica load; (c) packing here = LPT on aggregate load, there = greedy minimising the sum over steps of the worst rank load (complementarity-aware: it avoids stacking experts that peak in the same step); (d) DeepInfer vendors only the global policy, no group/node hierarchy; (e) neither adds an anti-affinity rule (two replicas of one expert may land on the same bin). In short DeepInfer's is EPLB's two-stage skeleton with the objective changed from mean load to per-step peak load.
   - *unaffected*: Comparison against D4 seed patch and DeepInfer policy.py L12-L140.
6. Hierarchical policy cannot apply to DS41: its routing config exposes 384 routed experts, 6 per token and sqrtsoftplus scoring with no n_group/topk_group field (NOT-FOUND in the official config.json and inference/config.json grep this session), so there are no expert groups to pack onto nodes. Only the global policy is relevant, which is also the one DeepInfer vendored.
   - *unaffected*: DS41 config grep.

### Dive corrections

- 2026-10-20 (wave 2, Stage-2b): ingested and dived in one pass; settled that the policy switch is divisibility-only, replicas are handed out one at a time to the hottest per-replica expert, packing is equal-cardinality LPT on a single aggregate vector, and DeepInfer's policy differs by objective (windowed per-step peak) rather than by structure; the repo has no numbers or dispatch code. 2026-10-20 second reader (independent agent): re-find 9/9; support yes 5 / partial 0 / no 0; corrections applied: none required.

### Verdict

Cite EPLB for the algorithm (greedy hottest-per-replica replication, LPT equal-cardinality packing, divisibility switch between hierarchical and global) and as the reference arm of RT-6. Do NOT cite it for any balance or speed number (none exists), for a dispatch or weight-movement design (absent), or as the policy DeepInfer runs (DeepInfer changed the objective to windowed per-step peak). Do NOT use it for CPU-vs-MI210 residency (RT-7 is a top-k coverage curve).

### Linked handoffs and actions

- updated `moe-routing-tap-and-locality-measurement.md`
- Recommended action (dive proposal, not an instruction): RT-6b: add EPLB-global as the reference arm of the offline replay
- Recommended action (dive proposal, not an instruction): RT-2c: trace-shape rider (order, prefill/decode tag, deepseek41 coverage)

## intake-2135#record — deepseek-ai/DeepEP - expert-parallel communication library (EP dispatch/combine, Engram remote fetch, redundant-expert weight exchange)

- **URL**: https://github.com/deepseek-ai/DeepEP
- **Verification**: dive-verified
- **Verdict**: adopt_patterns (novelty medium, relevance medium; categories: moe_optimization, inference_serving, memory_augmented, hardware_optimization)
- **Disposition**: monitor
- **Expanded from**: intake-2116#record

### Claims (as verified)

0. EngramBuffer storage (the D4 flag 'supports GPU/CPU storage'): each layer's table is a matrix of fixed-size entries (hidden x element-size bytes, BF16 or FP8-e4m3 with separately held scaling factors), sharded across ranks (each rank holds num_entries_per_layer rows per layer). With use_cpu_rdma_storage the shard is allocated with the CUDA virtual-memory API at location HOST_NUMA on the GPU's own NUMA node, mapped into the same address range as the GPU receive buffer, and registered for RDMA; otherwise it sits in HBM. `write` copies the tables in with cudaMemcpyAsync and asserts the source tensors are CUDA tensors, so a table passes through the device even when it lives in host memory. Nothing in the repo pages, pins explicitly, evicts or caches rows: 'CPU storage' means a NIC-readable host allocation, not a managed cache.
   - *narrowed*: D4 read the README phrase as storage support; code shows it is a NIC-registered host-NUMA allocation with no paging, pinning policy, eviction or cache, and tables are loaded through the device.
   - anchor `csrc/kernels/comm/symmetric.hpp:L153 @ b5a33ee` @ deepseek-ai/DeepEP @ b5a33eefda39485f438dc764695b71ba7b9249b6: "auto storage_prop = use_cpu_rdma_storage ? ctx.cpu_alloc_prop(true) : ctx.gpu_alloc_prop(true);"
   - anchor `csrc/kernels/comm/symmetric.hpp:L37 @ b5a33ee` @ deepseek-ai/DeepEP @ b5a33eefda39485f438dc764695b71ba7b9249b6: "return build_alloc_prop(CU_MEM_LOCATION_TYPE_HOST_NUMA, numa_idx, skip_fabric);"
   - anchor `csrc/buffers/engram.hpp:L125 @ b5a33ee` @ deepseek-ai/DeepEP @ b5a33eefda39485f438dc764695b71ba7b9249b6: "EP_HOST_ASSERT(storage.is_cuda() and storage.is_contiguous());"
1. How rows are fetched: the caller passes an int32 GPU tensor [num_layers, num_tokens, num_entries_per_token] of GLOBAL entry indices; one persistent kernel (one block per SM) has issue warps that, per layer, scan the indices, route each to its owning peer, batch up to 32 requests per warp and issue one-sided RDMA GET operations (gin.get) that land each row directly at its slot in a preallocated GPU receive buffer, ringing the doorbell only every few batches. The CPU is not involved after launch and no host-side gather happens.
   - *unaffected*: Mechanism read in engram_fetch.cuh and engram.hpp.
   - anchor `deep_ep/include/deep_ep/impls/engram/engram_fetch.cuh:L124 @ b5a33ee` @ deepseek-ai/DeepEP @ b5a33eefda39485f438dc764695b71ba7b9249b6: "gin.get<ncclTeamTagWorld, ncclCoopThread, ncclGin_SegmentMixed>("
   - anchor `csrc/buffers/engram.hpp:L164 @ b5a33ee` @ deepseek-ai/DeepEP @ b5a33eefda39485f438dc764695b71ba7b9249b6: "EP_HOST_ASSERT(indices.is_cuda() and indices.is_contiguous());"
   - anchor `deep_ep/buffers/engram.py:L160 @ b5a33ee` @ deepseek-ai/DeepEP @ b5a33eefda39485f438dc764695b71ba7b9249b6: "Issue the RDMA gets for all layers and return one wait hook per layer."
2. Prefetch and overlap: fetch() launches ONE issue kernel covering all layers (layer loop unrolled in-kernel, layer 0's requests go out first), records the last request of each layer, and returns one Python hook per layer; calling hook i enqueues a small wait kernel on the current stream and returns a view into the receive buffer. The tests time 'issue' and 'wait' separately. So the overlap depth is intra-forward only: issue everything at forward start, wait just before each layer needs its rows. There is no cross-step or speculative prefetch, no predictor, no residency tiering: it is exactly the 'hoist hash and fetch to forward start with one completion hook per layer' that D4 read in DeepInfer (deepseek.py:L177-L188), now seen from the library side.
   - *unaffected*: Confirms D4 finding of intra-forward overlap and no cross-step prefetch.
   - anchor `csrc/buffers/engram.hpp:L237 @ b5a33ee` @ deepseek-ai/DeepEP @ b5a33eefda39485f438dc764695b71ba7b9249b6: "launch_engram_fetch_wait("
   - anchor `tests/engram/test_engram.py:L121 @ b5a33ee` @ deepseek-ai/DeepEP @ b5a33eefda39485f438dc764695b71ba7b9249b6: "f'issue: {issue_t * 1e6:.1f} us, '"
3. Concurrency sizing is Little's law: get_theoretical_config sets queue-pair count = NIC read rate (45 or 90 million reads/s depending on fast-RDMA-atomic support) x an assumed 10 microsecond read round trip / 16 outstanding reads per QP / peers, then deepens the QP until all layers x tokens x entries requests fit (cap 32768 slots). The 10 us and the 45/90 Mpps are constants in the code, not measurements in the repo.
   - *unaffected*: Constants read from engram.hpp:get_theoretical_config.
   - anchor `csrc/buffers/engram.hpp:L279 @ b5a33ee` @ deepseek-ai/DeepEP @ b5a33eefda39485f438dc764695b71ba7b9249b6: "constexpr float kReadRTTSeconds = 10e-6f;"
   - anchor `csrc/buffers/engram.hpp:L287 @ b5a33ee` @ deepseek-ai/DeepEP @ b5a33eefda39485f438dc764695b71ba7b9249b6: "nic_read_mpps * 1e6f * kReadRTTSeconds / num_max_read_requests_per_qp / num_rdma_peers)), 1);"
4. FP8 handling: when entries are FP8 the scaling factors are NOT fetched over RDMA; every rank keeps them in GPU memory (the test replicates the full factor table so any rank can read any entry's factors locally; the kernel's scaling-factor warps read the shard via an NVLink-domain pointer), and the kernel writes them into a column-major TMA-aligned layout for the FP8 GEMM. Entry size must be a multiple of 64 bytes (kNumRDMAAlignmentBytes asserted in set_config).
   - *unaffected*: Test and kernel read.
   - anchor `tests/engram/test_engram.py:L48 @ b5a33ee` @ deepseek-ai/DeepEP @ b5a33eefda39485f438dc764695b71ba7b9249b6: "# Replicate the scaling factors so any rank can fetch any entry's factors locally"
5. Low-latency dispatch/combine, in one sentence: V2.5 no longer has a separate low-latency API; decode uses the same EPBuffer dispatch/combine as prefill and training but with do_cpu_sync=False, meaning receive counts stay on the GPU, outputs are sized to the configured capacity and the expert GEMMs read per-expert valid ranges from the handle, with the epilogue deferred so independent compute can overlap, all on persistent SM-resident NCCL-GIN kernels (README states zero-SM RDMA EP is unsupported). The dispatch/combine kernels themselves (dispatch.cuh, combine.cuh, hybrid_*.cuh) were only skimmed at the template signature level, so wire-protocol details (send ordering, FP8 dispatch packing, flag/atomic scheme) are NOT-FOUND here.
   - *narrowed*: Low-latency design only partly readable: unification and no-CPU-sync mode confirmed from README; kernel protocol not read.
   - anchor `README.md §Example use in model training and inference ¶1 @ b5a33ee` @ deepseek-ai/DeepEP @ b5a33eefda39485f438dc764695b71ba7b9249b6: "Training, inference prefilling, and inference decoding use the same `EPBuffer` dispatch and combine APIs."
   - anchor `README.md §Example use in model training and inference ¶2 @ b5a33ee` @ deepseek-ai/DeepEP @ b5a33eefda39485f438dc764695b71ba7b9249b6: "Set it to `False` when using GPU-side receive counts, as in decoding"
   - anchor `README.md §Notes @ b5a33ee` @ deepseek-ai/DeepEP @ b5a33eefda39485f438dc764695b71ba7b9249b6: "EP dispatch and combine require GPU SMs; zero-SM RDMA EP is not supported"
6. Redundant-expert weight movement (the runtime counterpart of EPLB): lb_prefetch_weights pushes original expert weights to redundant slots on peer ranks over NVLink before expert compute, asynchronously on the comm stream with an EventOverlap, driven by a caller-supplied redundancy_mapping [ranks, slots]; the planner and token rerouting are the caller's job, and the README confines replication to the NVLink domain.
   - *unaffected*: README only; lb_prefetch kernels not read (tests/ep/test_prefetch_weights.py not read).
   - anchor `README.md §Expert load balancing @ b5a33ee` @ deepseek-ai/DeepEP @ b5a33eefda39485f438dc764695b71ba7b9249b6: "`lb_prefetch_weights(redundant_expert_weights, expert_weights, redundancy_mapping)` pushes original expert weights to the requested redundant slots before expert computation."
7. Platform requirements as written: Hopper-or-newer NVIDIA GPUs, CUDA 13.1+ for the JIT, NCCL with GIN (Engram additionally needs ncclGinOptFlagsWarpGet), RDMA NIC (InfiniBand tested, RoCE theoretical). The README lists a ROCm backend (MORI, low-latency mode) and a PCIe-kernel Hybrid-EP only as separate branches, not read here.
   - *unaffected*: README and compiled.cuh.
   - anchor `README.md §Notes @ b5a33ee` @ deepseek-ai/DeepEP @ b5a33eefda39485f438dc764695b71ba7b9249b6: "Engram requires a NCCL build providing `ncclGinOptFlagsWarpGet`"
   - anchor `deep_ep/include/deep_ep/common/compiled.cuh:L68 @ b5a33ee` @ deepseek-ai/DeepEP @ b5a33eefda39485f438dc764695b71ba7b9249b6: "constexpr int kNumRDMAAlignmentBytes = 64;"

### Dive corrections

- 2026-10-20 (wave 2, Stage-2b): ingested and dived in one pass; settled the D4-flagged EngramBuffer: CPU storage = NIC-registered host-NUMA CUDA VMM allocation, rows fetched by GPU-issued one-sided gets in a single multi-layer launch with a per-layer wait hook, no cross-step prefetch and no paging/cache policy; low-latency EP = same EPBuffer in no-CPU-sync mode (kernel protocol not read). 2026-10-20 second reader (independent agent): re-find 17/17; support yes 8 / partial 0 / no 0; corrections applied: none required.

### Verdict

Cite DeepEP for: how EngramBuffer stores and fetches rows (GPU-initiated one-sided gets from a NIC-registered HBM or host-NUMA shard, one launch for all layers, per-layer wait hook, intra-forward overlap only, no paging or cache policy) and the Little's-law QP sizing. Do NOT carry its latency constants, its (absent) performance numbers, or any claim about low-latency kernel internals beyond the README-level no-CPU-sync decode mode. It does not change DS41-B9: a CPU-resident engram needs no transfer machinery; it informs only the shape of B9b and the optional MI210 pricing task T7b.

## intake-2136#record — DeepSeek V4.1 Flash 大规模推理部署实践 (DeepSeek V4.1 Flash large-scale inference deployment practice; Zhihu post by DeepSeek-AI accompanying the DeepInfer release)

- **URL**: https://zhuanlan.zhihu.com/p/2_095_560_295_930_508_953
- **Verification**: dive-verified
- **Verdict**: adopt_patterns (novelty medium, relevance medium; categories: inference_serving, kv_cache, speculative_decoding, cost_aware_routing, moe_optimization)
- **Disposition**: integrated
- **Expanded from**: intake-2116#record

### Claims (as verified)

0. KV cache store placement and hit path (vendor design, matches the Q&A). Block KV (128-token pages) lives on a dedicated 3FS storage cluster, not on compute-node memory, so every prefill machine can hit any prefix with no prefix-affinity routing, storage scales independently of compute, and cache survives compute failures (compute fails more than an order of magnitude more often than storage). The hit path has three stages and only the first is on the critical path: hash-chain prefix match over metadata only on the local CHECK-role kvcache-store (hit length must be known BEFORE routing, so it cannot be hidden), then asynchronous fetch 3FS to host shm in a separate process, then per-layer H2D swap-in overlapped with forward compute (done with GDR/RDMA-NIC because the CUDA copy engine is slow on fine-grained KV block copies).
   - *unaffected*: Matches the Q&A KV-store section; carried once, no new content beyond the Q&A except the explicit statement that match must precede routing.
   - anchor `zhihu.md:L132 (section KVCache Storage / Block KVCache)` @ zhihu.md sha256 a7c0e06a3df3: "相比多级存储系统，独立的存储集群使得所有 prefill 机器能命中任何前缀，避免前缀亲和和负载均衡路由策略冲突"
   - anchor `zhihu.md:L136 (hit path)` @ zhihu.md sha256 a7c0e06a3df3: "只查元数据，但命中长度需在路由前进行计算，无法掩盖"
   - anchor `zhihu.md:L136 (swap-in)` @ zhihu.md sha256 a7c0e06a3df3: "由于 CUDA copy engine 对于 KVCache Block 细粒度的拷贝性能不佳，换入使用 GDR 实现，用 RDMA 网卡就能打满 PCIe 带宽，并且不占 SM"
1. NEW vs Q&A (vendor-reported online data): the SWA window KV is deliberately NOT persisted. It stays in the memory of the machine that produced it, indexed by a logically unified, key-partitioned in-memory directory on the Entrypoints; a miss locally pulls cross-machine. The post reports the SWA pool turns over with TTL about 180 s and hit rate above 98% (online data; no denominator, window or traffic mix given), and misses are repaired by Encoder SWA Bounded Replay.
   - *unaffected*: New to the lane: not in the Q&A. Vendor online data without denominators.
   - anchor `zhihu.md:L140 (section SWA KVCache)` @ zhihu.md sha256 a7c0e06a3df3: "线上数据显示，其TTL约 180 秒，命中率在 98% 以上"
2. NEW vs Q&A: the V4 to V4.1 change is motivated by cost. In V4 the SWA KV was persisted at two positions and a miss recomputed layers x window tokens exactly, so SWA ate nearly half of persistent KV capacity while giving little hit benefit (block KV is long-tail reused for days, SWA only for minutes inside a live session). The V4 report Zero SWA Caching (no persistence, exact recompute) was judged too expensive in production and NOT adopted; V4.1 uses SWA Bounded Replay instead.
   - *unaffected*: New to the lane: the V4 versus V4.1 capacity and Zero-SWA-Caching rationale is not in the Q&A; the V4.1 report (intake-2117#record) is the primary for the architectural side, this post is the serving-side motive.
   - anchor `zhihu.md:L158 (section SWA Bounded Replay)` @ zhihu.md sha256 a7c0e06a3df3: "SWA KVCache 占去持久 KVCache 近一半容量"
   - anchor `zhihu.md:L158` @ zhihu.md sha256 a7c0e06a3df3: "因其代价在生产部署中过高，在V4.1推理系统中未实际采用"
3. SWA Bounded Replay: replay only the last 128 tokens and truncate the attention window to the replay segment (from position s, query i attends keys max(s, i-127)..i), giving an APPROXIMATE SWA KV at constant cost independent of context length and layer count. Encoder form: on an SWA miss recompute the last 128 tokens of the hit prefix with the uncached suffix; block KV reused untouched. Decoder form (CED): exact rebuild would run the last 2560 tokens through 20 decoder layers; instead only the last 128 prompt tokens are recomputed there, used for decode only, never entering the prefix cache, which nearly halves total prefill compute (qualitative, no number). The post gives NO quality measurement of the approximation.
   - *unaffected*: Same as Q&A and seed D2. Post adds no quality measurement, so D2 finding that approximation quality is unmeasured stands.
   - anchor `zhihu.md:L160` @ zhihu.md sha256 a7c0e06a3df3: "从位置 s 起回放，位置 i 的 query 只和 max(s, i − 127) 到 i 区间的 key 计算，重算得到近似的 SWA KVCache"
   - anchor `zhihu.md:L163 (Decoder SWA Bounded Replay)` @ zhihu.md sha256 a7c0e06a3df3: "精确重建要 20 个 decoder 层跑最后 2560 个 token；改为每次 prefill 在 decoder 层只重算 prompt 末尾 128 个 token，产出仅供 decode、不进前缀缓存，总 prefill 计算量近乎减半"
4. Hit policy (identical to the Q&A): hit length is capped at L-134 (128 window + 6 speculative backtrack); block KV gives hit length L1 (multiple of 128); if an SWA KV exists in [L1-128, L1] the hit is L1, otherwise L1-128 and the last block is recomputed.
   - *unaffected*: Identical to the Q&A hit policy; no implementing code is open (seed D2/D3).
   - anchor `zhihu.md:L146 (section 命中策略)` @ zhihu.md sha256 a7c0e06a3df3: "最大的命中长度最多为 L - 134（窗口 128 + 投机回退 6）"
   - anchor `zhihu.md:L146` @ zhihu.md sha256 a7c0e06a3df3: "假如在 L1 - 128 至 L1 长度范围内，存在 SWA KVCache 则命中长度为 L1，否则命中长度为 L1 - 128"
5. Prefill load balancer (4 layers): requests bucketed by total input length into <16K / 16-64K / 64-256K / >=256K with engines per tier re-split each round by that tier compute-quota demand share; across DP groups engines PULL by remaining compute-quota with no prefix affinity; inside a group each request goes to the lowest-quota DP rank; inside a GPU requests are FIFO and the chunk size is not fixed but solved from a per-step time budget (ns) by inverting a pre-fitted quadratic cost(q,c). The post defines q as the number of UNCACHED tokens of the sequence and c as the prefix length already computed; no coefficients, fit procedure, budget value or round length are given.
   - *narrowed*: Narrows the Q&A wording: the Q&A says q is the number of sequence tokens, the post says uncached tokens (q) versus already-computed prefix (c). Q&A-only details: 32768-token cap, all-reduce MIN, per-tier quota caps. Post-only: nothing on coefficients. Seed D3 NOT-FOUND-IN-CODE finding for the cost model stands: neither text gives coefficients or the fit procedure.
   - anchor `zhihu.md:L88 (section Prefill Load Balancer)` @ zhihu.md sha256 a7c0e06a3df3: "`q` = 序列中未命中的 token 数，`c` = 这条序列已经算完的前缀长度"
   - anchor `zhihu.md:L90` @ zhihu.md sha256 a7c0e06a3df3: "按输入总长分桶为 4 档：<16K / 16-64K / 64-256K / ≥256K，每档分到的引擎台数按该档上一轮的 compute-quota 需求占比动态重分"
   - anchor `zhihu.md:L91` @ zhihu.md sha256 a7c0e06a3df3: "由于任何引擎都能命中任何前缀，不做前缀亲和路由"
6. Decode load balancer: routes uniformly across all GPUs of all decode instances and balances two dimensions, request count (sets per-step batch size) and total KV length (sets attention cost); heuristic is short requests to low-concurrency high-total-KV GPUs and long requests to low-total-KV GPUs, with the algorithm given only by citation to DualPath intra-group scheduling. No code is open.
   - *unaffected*: Identical to the Q&A; algorithm only by DualPath citation (already in the lane as intake-2119#record).
   - anchor `zhihu.md:L97 (section Decode Load Balancer)` @ zhihu.md sha256 a7c0e06a3df3: "同时均衡请求数和 KVCache 总长度两个维度，前者决定每步批大小，后者决定注意力部分的代价"
   - anchor `zhihu.md:L97` @ zhihu.md sha256 a7c0e06a3df3: "启发式地将短请求发给并发较小且 KVCache 总长偏大的卡，长请求发给 KVCache 总长较小的卡"
7. Autoscaling and fault tolerance: prefill instance count follows uncached-token volume, decode follows running-request count, adjustments rate-limited and bounded; a decode instance told to go down gets a 1 min grace, then unfinished requests plus their KV are forwarded to other instances in one shot; redundancy is held as ready spare machines plus a hysteresis rule (no scale-in while quota utilisation exceeds 0.8); restarts take minutes so fault tolerance comes from quota redundancy plus request-level rerouting, and a large-EP instance with a failed GPU restarts with EP resized to its healthy GPU count. Numeric rate limits and bounds are not stated.
   - *unaffected*: Matches the Q&A; both omit the numeric rate limits and bounds.
   - anchor `zhihu.md:L114 (section 自动扩缩容)` @ zhihu.md sha256 a7c0e06a3df3: "Prefill 通过统计未命中 token 数判断所需实例数目，decode 通过在计算的请求数判断所需实例数目，调整有限速和上下界"
   - anchor `zhihu.md:L115` @ zhihu.md sha256 a7c0e06a3df3: "配额占用率高于 0.8 时禁止缩容"
   - anchor `zhihu.md:L116` @ zhihu.md sha256 a7c0e06a3df3: "错误重启在分钟量级，容错依赖配额冗余 + 请求级重新路由的组合机制，而不是靠缩短实例重启时间"
8. DSpark: relative to DFlash the drafter adds a low-rank logit bias that depends only on the previous token id (verified in the official reference model.py: a rank-256 Markov head fed with the previous output id, whose embedding also feeds the confidence head). In serving, variable-length verification: draft emits 5 tokens, a Confidence Head predicts each draft-token acceptance rate, tokens are sorted by expected acceptance and the prefix subset with maximum effective throughput against a profiled hardware throughput curve is verified. With async scheduling the CPU fixes only the total budget and a TileLang GPU kernel (cumulative product, global top-k sort, fused remap scatter) recomputes per-request lengths into a pinned buffer.
   - *unaffected*: Previous-token low-rank logit bias is confirmed in model.py and mirrored in our port; the varlen mechanism and GPU-recompute split match seed D1 (which found the open-release varlen path default off, not contradicted: the post describes the production deployment where it is evidently on).
   - anchor `zhihu.md:L167 (section DSpark投机解码)` @ zhihu.md sha256 a7c0e06a3df3: "相比 DFlash 在输出的 base logits 上叠加只依赖前一 token id 的低秩 logit bias"

### Dive corrections

- 2026-10-20 (wave 2, Stage-2b): ingested and dived in one pass; settled that the post is the parent of the Q&A and mostly restated by it (read in full from disk, images not read). No contradiction found with seed D1 (varlen default off applies to the open release) or D3 (cost model absent from code; the post gives no coefficients either). 2026-10-20 second reader (independent agent): re-find 28/28; support yes 13 / partial 0 / no 0 (claims 9 and 11 compare with the DeepInfer Q&A outside their anchors; unchecked part noted). Main dropped a malformed bare-list depends_on [intake-2116#record]: the post is the Q&A's parent text, so its claims do not rest on intake-2116#record's. 2026-10-20 STAGE-2 CLOSE-OUT: dive-surfaced github.com/deepseek-ai/3FS DECLINED per the dive recommendation: distributed RDMA filesystem at 100 PiB scale has no single-host consumer; the transferable lessons (EC read fan-out, LRU GC under a watermark, SWA state RAM-only with a short TTL) are recorded in this entry. Carried to the Stage-3 plan for operator confirmation. 2026-10-21 RE-ENCOUNTER (session intake-deepseek-prodstack-20261021, operator-pasted commentary on this post, minted as intake-2214#record): same URL, not re-minted. Re-fetched today through r.jina.ai (sha256 a87966c6255d; 49854 B; identical to the 2026-10-20 copy after stripping the expiring zhida entity-link tokens; direct curl 403, api.zhihu.com 40362, web.archive.org and archive.ph blocked). Article statements the relayed summary carries that this entry's key_claims omit, verified in the re-fetched text: parallelism spec (prefill EP2-8/DP2-8 on 8-card machines, decode EP64-128 super-nodes, L13/L44-L45); Vision Encoder deployed single-card on the cheapest available GPU with its own load balancer (random machine, then the card with the fewest image patches, L46/L101); structured output compiled on separate SO machines, asynchronously, fetched before the request enters the engine (L184-L187); async scheduling pipeline (L175-L176); the three design goals incl. KV retention of 72 h or more (L12-L14). NOT in the article: the PP-vs-EP prefill position (X author, intake-2214#record claim 4). Garbled in the relay: 'decode instances own block metadata' (article: decode instances write Block KV to the store, L136).

### Verdict

This post is the parent text of the vendor Q&A in intake-2116#record and adds little engine detail; cite it for (a) the SWA-state-not-persisted design with the vendor-reported 180 s / above 98% figures and the V4-versus-V4.1 capacity argument, (b) the definition q = uncached tokens in the prefill cost(q,c) model, (c) the DSpark previous-token low-rank logit-bias definition (confirmed against model.py and our port), and (d) the traffic-class TTL structure (agent 3 d, chatbot 7 d, window state minutes) as a prompt for our own HSF-3 gap data. Do NOT carry: the 155 trillion tokens/day or 38 USD per card-hour as efficiency evidence, the +6% as an online or live A/B result, either moe_lb prefill figure to three decimals (the two vendor texts disagree), or any 3FS bandwidth number as relevant to a single host. No source claim here is an EPYC measurement; the cost(q,c) model, decode balancing heuristic and bounded-replay quality remain unverifiable from open code.
